#6865. Inside a Modern Hamptons Home With Views in Nearly Every Direction

Leroy Street Studio designed this Water Mill retreat around a large blended family—and a landscape that changes with every season.

It’s hard to pick a favorite time of year at Peninsula House. Nicknamed for its location on a point overlooking Mecox Bay in Water Mill, New York, the residence was designed by the architecture firm Leroy Street Studio to capitalize on the site’s 270-degree views. An array of indoor and outdoor vantage points capture the ever-changing cinematic interplay of light and water as well as nature’s shifts from season to season.

The owners, a couple with a large blended family, wanted a multigenerational gathering spot that they and their young daughter could share with the husband’s adult children and their spouses as well as friends. From the outset, the clients made it clear they were looking for out-of-the-ordinary architecture that is thoughtfully, creatively integrated into its unique setting. “They really liked the idea of doing something that was countercurrent for the Hamptons,” says Morgan Hare, the Leroy Street founding partner who headed the project.

Adopting a pinwheel-type plan, the striking 14,000-square-foot house features multiple wings radiating from a central core. These spaces vary in scale and height, and some feature deep overhangs, providing shade and amplifying the design’s dynamic horizontality. “The oversailing roofs create a solar brim that helps keep the rooms cool in the summer,” says Alex Witko, a senior associate at Leroy Street who served as the project lead. “They also amplify the views, including from the primary bedroom, where you can just sit and watch the herons on the nearby spit of land that is a protected wildlife preserve.”

Softening the formal rigor, most of the house is topped with lushly planted green roofs and terraces that offer distinct vistas, while the extensively glazed exterior is clad in handmade white brick on the lower levels and dark-stained wood above. The black and white palette is one that Leroy Street, which has designed dozens of waterfront homes in the Hamptons and elsewhere, has rarely used previously. “The dark wood, somewhat counterintuitively, blends into and dematerializes against the vegetation,” Hare says, noting how the house opens on all sides to tree-shaded terraces and courtyards.

Landscape architect Ed Hollander and his firm Hollander Design collaborated on the 1.7-acre property’s outdoor living spaces, while outfitting the gardens with what he calls “maritime habitat” plantings, a mix of native and non-native species “that are adapted to this sunny, salty, breezy environment.”

Everything in the house’s design was conceived around creating a seamless flow—visual and literal—between inside and outside. The main entry consists of a two-story glass-walled gallery that connects the two largest wings, providing views from the front walkway through the house to a rear garden planted with ginkgo trees and the bay beyond. “In the morning, the sun actually comes up through that ginkgo grove and reflects off the entry hall’s glass pendant lights,” notes Hollander. “Then in the evening, the lights become like starlight, these magic things that dance against the trees.”

Inside, the light-and-dark palette continues with the white artisanal brick, expanses of warm plaster, and pale oak and stone offset by inky-hued wood and blackened-steel accents. Throughout, the predominantly new and custom furnishings, overseen by Eleanor Donnelly of Stelle Lomont Rouhani Architects, stick to a sculptural, pared-down aesthetic, complemented by a contemporary art collection that includes works by Teresita Fernández, Sarah Morris, and Adam McEwen. Elevated details like bespoke integrated door pulls and closets with facings in faux-shagreen or mottled resin add subtle character.

The horseshoe-shaped main stairwell is one of few rounded architectural elements in the house. With floating treads and a minimalist oculus at the top, there’s a play of light, shadow, and form that shifts as one moves between floors.

Reaching the home’s dramatic second-story rooftop terrace requires taking a separate, private flight of stairs. A seductive spot for sunsets and stargazing, it features a seating area with a fire bowl surrounded by wispy native grasses and plants.

While a major appeal of this house is all of its intimate spaces for quiet escape, equally important was having versatile areas for gathering in larger groups. That might mean bringing two dozen guests together around the formal dining tables that can be combined and separated as needed or congregating in the various outdoor spaces around the expansive pool area.

Step Inside This Waterfront Hamptons Home

Hare notes that while the firm has designed plenty of residences for extended families, they have typically taken the form of compounds with separate structures. “This is the first one we've done that’s multigenerational and all in one building,” he says. “It was a big part of our mission—and, ultimately, the success of the house.”

ELLE Decor features director Stephen Wallis has spent three decades covering art, design, and culture. He was previously the consulting features editor for Galerie and held top positions at Architectural Digest and Departures. His writing has appeared in numerous publications, including The New York Times, T magazine, WSJ magazine, Esquire, Town & Country, Veranda, and Cultured. He is the author of Fontainebleau, a history of the iconic Miami Beach hotel, and Jim Thompson: Beyond Silk, Beyond Thailand.

Once a participant finishes, they're generally on their merry way.

If you've ever swiped a finger through the frosting of a cake you were explicitly told not to taste, you know being told you shouldn't do something can make you want to do it even more. Turns out, the same psychology can carry over into our bed chambers, too.

Given the taboo surrounding condomless sex with multiple partners, it's not hard to see how cumdumping could scratch that particular erotic itch.

For some queer men, the appeal goes beyond the thrill of breaking the rules. It's about reclaiming the very sexuality that has long been stigmatized and policed.

Cumdumping and otherwise “having condom-free sex with multiple strangers is often judged as ‘reckless’ behavior or even pathologized as ‘self-harm’,” he says.

Advances in HIV prevention have also made it possible for more people to explore fantasies that once felt too risky to act on. “Before PrEP, cumdump was a popular fantasy, but few practiced it,” Neves says. So, nowadays, for some queer men, cumdumping can feel like a celebration of queer sexuality on their own terms. “Many cumdumps I spoke to mentioned sexual freedom as one of the main arousing elements of it,” he says.

Of course, queer men aren't the only ones whose sex lives have been policed. Queer and heterosexual women and people of other genders have faced their own forms of sexual stigma, including purity politics and slut-shaming. For some, having sex with multiple people at once—in a cumdump event or otherwise—can feel like taking a red marker to those tired sexual scripts and reclaiming their sexuality on their own terms.

...and again and again and again. In Neves's 2026 research, participants shared that another part of the appeal was the validation of being desired by multiple men, each of whom chose them as the recipient of their grand finale.

“When men ejaculate, there is a refractory period, which means that the sexual activity is over for them,” explains Neves. “Some cumdumps feel very aroused at knowing tops chose them to conclude their sexual act, or to relieve their horniness,” he says.

The advent of X, Grindr, and other social and hook-up platforms makes it easier than ever for cumdump enthusiasts to find one another. And, in countries where PrEP is accessible, Neves reports that communities are emerging around the practice.

Some cumdumps even affectionately refer to themselves as “community holes,” a term that reflects their desire to be sexually available to other men within the queer community. For queer folks historically excluded from traditional spaces of belonging, that sense of community can be especially meaningful.

Each additional partner adds another potential source of exposure, De Lin says. “The risk isn’t necessarily about the quantity of semen itself, but about factors such as the STI status of each partner, the type of sexual contact, and the prevention strategies being used,” she says.

Anal intercourse generally carries a higher STI risk compared to other sexual activities, given that without proper warm-up and lubrication, repeated penetration may also cause microtrauma to the delicate rectal tissues, potentially increasing susceptibility to certain infections, she explains. First, get acquainted with a little thing called pre-exposure prophylaxis (PrEP) for HIV. “PrEP is one of the most effective tools available for reducing the risk of HIV acquisition, particularly for individuals who anticipate condomless sex with multiple partners,” De-Lin tells Cosmopolitan. “When taken or administered as prescribed, PrEP reduces the risk of HIV-negative folks sexually acquiring HIV by approximately 99%.” So, if you plan to rack up loads faster than the U.S. government accumulates national debt, talk to your health care provider about whether PrEP is right for you.

PrEP is a powerhouse, but it doesn't prevent gonorrhea, chlamydia, or syphilis, so you'll need a few other safer-sex strategies in your arsenal, too. Vaccination—including for HPV, hepatitis B, hepatitis A, and mpox—is an important and sometimes overlooked component of sexual-health prevention, she says. “Condoms remain effective at reducing the risk of HIV and many other STIs. Even if someone chooses not to use condoms for every activity, using them selectively can still reduce certain exposures,” she adds.

Finally, don’t forget to make regular STI screening a regular piece of your safer-sex puzzle. “Individuals with multiple partners or ongoing higher-risk exposures may benefit from screening every three months, rather than annually,” says De-Lin. If your cumdumping includes oral and anal sex, be sure to ask for site-specific tests of the throat and rectum, too, she says, “since urine or genital testing alone can miss extragenital infections.”

If you're partnered, the first step is getting comfortable talking about your freaky fantasies. “When it comes to sharing sexual fantasies with a partner, no matter what they are about, the starting point is normalizing conversations about sex within the relationship,” says Lehmiller. Face it, it's going to be pretty darn hard to talk about cumdumping if you can't even talk about sex.

If you're nervous about bringing up the fantasy, and especially if you're in a sexually closed relationship, Lehmiller recommends starting with something that won’t feel so threatening to The Sanctity of The Relationship (like, say, trying rider-on-top or piss play!).

Getting specific about what makes the fantasy hot for you can also help your partner understand where you're coming from, especially because they likely haven’t thought about cumdumping with quite as much zhuzh or detail as you.

Your partner might be intrigued by some elements of the fantasy but not others—and that's OK! “We don’t always need to enact a fantasy exactly as it appears in our minds to be fulfilled,” Lehmiller explains. It’s all about working as a team to find the overlap in your Fantasy Venn Diagram.

#6862. So Is Lanterns Canceled Or What?

It was one of the best new HBO shows in recent memory, but it seems like that won’t matter at all.

You know what happens when you blow up a universe? You’re left sifting through the rubble. Since the news that James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran have been ousted as co-CEOs and cochairmen of DC Studios, there are many questions about the future of their DC Universe. Chief among them is Lanterns. So is Lanterns canceled? That’s what everyone wants to know.

The confusion started when Variety published its report about the exit of Gunn and Safran on November 10. The brunt of the story details how Gunn and Safran’s leadership of the studio was a modest, but not big enough, success for Warner Bros. (and now Skydance) brass. “Efforts to right the ship were mixed at best—with tentpoles and television shows that didn’t always generate enough excitement to justify their price tags,” wrote Variety. “Sources suggest the thinking was they spent big—and lost too much—on projects that just weren’t resonating with audiences.”

In the story, Variety explored the studio’s TV efforts, which we should note have plenty of asterisks. Under Gunn and Safran’s leadership, DC Studios released The Penguin, a spin-off of The Batman that won awards; the caveat is that it was already in development before Gunn and Safran took over. There was also the second season of Peacemaker, a series created by Gunn (as a spin-off of his 2021 movie The Suicide Squad) but whose creation also, weirdly, predates Gunn’s reign over DC. It was also the second season of a television show that premiered three years earlier.

This leads to Lanterns, the first DC show to grow from conception to air under Gunn and Safran. In Variety’s original version of its story, the outlet’s phrasing about the fate of Lanterns made the Internet jump, as it suggested the show, which cultivated a dedicated audience late in the summer, was canceled. “An insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show didn’t get renewed for a second season,” Variety wrote.

Hours later, Variety changed its story to its current phrasing (the new words in bold): “Yet an insider at the company suggests the crime drama isn’t quite capturing the zeitgeist, pointing out the show has yet to be renewed for a second season.”

Variety also added the same source, saying, “People cannot wait to make another season of a hit show.”

You can see why there’s confusion. Gunn and Safran’s dismissal from DC was sudden, blindsiding even the men themselves (according to trades like The Hollywood Reporter) at a time when DC Studios seemed to be in a steady rhythm. Sure, this year’s Supergirl was a fumble, but last year’s Superman had gotten the refreshed DCU off on the right foot, and the recent mid-budget horror thriller Clayface grossed a clean $100 million. Though none of the Gunn/Safran–era movies were eye-watering successes, you also can’t measure long-term impact and sustained popularity of a franchise in box-office milestones alone. That’s not how art works.

It’s especially confusing with Lanterns. Back in October, outlets like Variety reported strong viewership figures for the series on HBO and HBO Max. In Skydance’s own third-quarter earnings call, the studio touted Lanterns as a rousing success, with an average of 22 million global viewers, “among HBO Max’s strongest launches” ever for the platform. To now read from insider sources claiming Lanterns wasn’t “quite capturing the zeitgeist” is downright absurd and frankly an insult to anyone’s intelligence.

Is Lanterns canceled? The good news is it isn’t. The bad news is that it isn’t canceled yet. Lanterns was a key part of Gunn’s grand plans for the DCU. Now that he’s been removed—and his future at the company unclear, despite more projects in development under his guidance—the fate of Lanterns is uncertain, as is the whole DCU.

A likely scenario is that Skydance will burn through all the remaining DC projects and dump them on HBO Max—stuff like Gunn’s Superman sequel Man of Tomorrow, the mockumentary comedy The People vs. Gorilla Grodd, and the animated film Dynamic Duo, which is said to be a highly experimental project using puppetry and animation. Lanterns season 2 could be among them, perhaps fast-tracked to HBO Max, where it would get a swift cancellation regardless of its performance (especially now that audiences will know not to get invested), or it simply doesn’t get made at all. But it’s anyone’s guess what will actually happen.

Whether audiences will get to see John Stewart be the Green Lantern is now in the hands of new studio leaders like Jeremy Latcham, who will replace Gunn and Safran starting December 1. We’ll see if Latcham leads with willpower or through fear.

#6858. The Best Sci-Fi Movies of 2026 (So Far)

Before Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three compete at the box office, Godzilla returns to remind everyone why he’s called the “King of the Monsters.”

Two of the biggest sci-fi movies of the year are upon us, when Avengers: Doomsday and Dune: Part Three both hit theaters on December 18. We’ll see if either multimillion-dollar blockbuster has the juice to compete with this year’s crop—namely Project Hail Mary, which kicked off the year on a very high note for sci-fi films.

Directed by Phil Lord and Christopher Miller, the Ryan Gosling-starring astronaut film that plays like Interstellar but if Matthew McConaughey had a talking rock for a friend was easily one of the best sci-fi films of the past five years. We also saw The End of Oak Street earlier this summer, and Steven Spielberg returned to the world of aliens with Disclosure Day. Plus, Godzilla Minus Zero just stomped into theaters to remind everyone just why they call him the “King of the Monsters” right before Dunesday.

Until then, these are the best science-fiction movies of 2026.

The atomic monster returns to the screen following the success of Godzilla Minus One, as director Takashi Yamazaki sets up another stellar sequel for Godzilla to pulverize in his fight against King Gedorah. “In this series, Godzilla is a metaphor for war,” the filmmaker told Esquire. “What I wanted to do with this one is see how this family recovers and thrives after having suffered, and then see them survive another challenge.” Plus, leveling some cities with giant monster fights doesn’t hurt either.

The End of Oak Street is the best dinosaur film since Jurassic Park—and yes, I am including all the Jurassic Park sequels in that ranking. David Robert Mitchell’s sci-fi film about a neighborhood that is semi-transported back to prehistoric times stars Ewan McGregor and Anne Hathaway—in yet another great performance this year following The Odyssey—as the parents navigate their new Jumanji-like world.

Don’t let the three-hour runtime fool you, this South Korean monster film by director Na Hong-jin is the best thrill ride of the year. The plot follows police chief Ko Bum-seok as he investigates a brutally destructive monster that has seemingly emerged from the woods to terrify the port-side town of Hope Harbor.

Esquire’s Eric Francisco said it best when he described the beat-’em-up action flick based on the popular video game series as “incredibly stupid fun.” Noah Centineo and Andrew Koji star as friendly rivals Ryu and Ken alongside an oddball and eccentric cast including David Dastmalchian, Roman Reigns, Cody Rhodes, Andrew Schulz, Eric Andre, Orville Peck, 50 Cent, and Jason Momoa as the game’s various fighters. Still, amid all the chaos, it’s probably Callina Liang’s dedication to Chun-Li that shines through the brightest.

Following Barbarian and Weapons, Zach Cregger’s take on Resident Evil marks the horror director as 3-for-3 in my book. The contained yet still incredibly terrifying story follows a medical courier (Austin Abrams) on the night that a violent virus turns everyone into crazed, mutated killers. While the sci-fi/horror film definitely packs in the scares, I was still surprised how funny and accurate the film was to its video game source material. Among all the video game adaptations over the past few years, Resident Evil felt the most like actually playing the game.

One of the biggest movies of 2026 is, without question, Project Hail Mary. Based on the 2021 novel by Andy Weir, the smash hit follows a middle school science teacher (Ryan Gosling) who awakes with amnesia aboard a spacecraft light-years away from Earth. The newest movie from comedy giants Phil Lord and Christopher Miller was the right movie at the right time, its release accidentally coinciding with the Artemis II mission to the moon. That it was still playing in theaters more than a month after its release—an eternity in today’s theatrical landscape—speaks volumes about its out-of-this-world appeal.

The man who invented the modern summer blockbuster came back to theaters in 2026 with his first sci-fi in years: Disclosure Day. Emily Blunt and Josh O’Connor costar as two people who try to reveal the existence of extraterrestrial life, while the government tries to suppress the truth. It may not have been the glorious throwback to the days of E.T. and Close Encounters, but Disclosure Day gets by on career-best work by Blunt and another immaculate score by John Williams.

Alan Ritchson flexes his leading-man muscles in War Machine, a Netflix original that’s actually kinda worth watching. The Reacher star leads the movie as a staff sergeant for the Army Rangers who encounters a massively overpowered alien force during a training exercise. Sure, you’ve seen movies like this before—Predator, anyone?—but this throwback action flick is the best kind of turn-your-brain-off junk food we secretly crave.

You don’t have to be an overgrown ’80s baby to dig Masters of the Universe. This deeply affectionate reboot of the Reagan-era toy and TV franchise once again tells the story of Prince Adam (Nicholas Galitzine), who must save his fallen home of Eternia from the evil Skeletor (Jared Leto). The franchise started out as a cheap competitor to Star Wars, but the 2026 reboot is anything but a pale copy, with its dazzling visual design, snappy humor, and, most of all, a moving story about reasserting one’s place in the world.

How much you want to consider Marvel superhero movies “sci-fi” depends on you, but we safely consider radioactive spiders, mutant telepaths, and gamma-infused monsters to be firmly in the realm of science fiction. The fourth Tom Holland–led Spider-Man movie sees the web slinger working on his own until an invisible menace with the power to hijack people’s consciousness threatens everyone he still loves. It’s a box-office (Hulk) smash, but Spider-Man: Brand New Day remembers that the true story lies in the hearts of heroes.

If Guy Ritchie made Looper, it might look like Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice. Emerging out of SXSW, the Hulu exclusive follows a gangster (Vince Vaughn) who, after regretting murdering his friend (James Marsden), uses a time machine to stop his past self. There are an awful lot of action comedies nowadays, but Mike & Nick & Nick & Alice stands out from the pack with some novel twists on well-worn time-travel formulas.

Sydney Chandler (Alien: Earth) and Takehiro Hira (Giri/Haji) hit the road in this tender and emotional sci-fi road trip from first-time director Brian Tetsuro Ivie. Set in a speculative near future in which one’s consciousness can be uploaded into the cloud, Anima sees Chandler star as an unemployed engineer who drives a wealthy and terminally ill man (Hira) across Connecticut in the hopes of finding his estranged son before the end of his life. Anima premiered to rave reviews at SXSW earlier this year and may soon find wide-release distribution.

Animated children’s films about woodland creatures are usually a mixed bag. For every Bambi or The Wild Robot, you’ll find a dozen films trying to become the next Over the Hedge. Hoppers dodges the repetitive trend by magically turning a young girl into a woodland creature, and the box-office numbers prove that the gimmick has paid off so far. The film follows Mabel Tanaka, who transports her consciousness into a robot beaver. She wants to rally the animals to protect their wilderness from becoming a highway. It’s a crazy idea. But considering the way we’re headed in 2026, I’m down for anything.

This SXSW darling stars Lewis Pullman and Maya Hawke as a couple with the ability to literally shape their environment. After attending a couples-counseling session run by a set of magic wonder twins, the two on-again, off-again lovers learn that their emotions have dire effects on the world around them. The silly, video-game-like premise isn’t Wishful Thinking’s only draw, however. At its heart, director Graham Parkes’s feature debut really touches on a question we all ask ourselves. How do we change for the person we love without sacrificing what makes us who we are?

Ralph Fiennes’s Dr. Ian Kelson experiments with some real science fiction in 28 Years Later: The Bone Temple, which is one of the first zombie flicks I’ve ever seen to reckon with the idea that a cure is physically attainable. This second entry in a planned sequel trilogy to the original 28 Days Later film also features Jack O’Connell (Sinners) as a maniacal cult leader styled after Jimmy Savile—just tearing up the apocalyptic landscape with Danny Boyle’s zany, violent humor.

#6856. You Can Definitely Do These Easy Nail Art Designs at Home

Take it from a fellow amateur manicurist.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

Ah, the at-home manicure. Nail artists make it look so easy when you’re in the salon, but get in front of a file and dotting tool at home? Suddenly, things can get a little shaky. I’ve technically been doing my own nails since I was about six, but really started leaning into trying to get better at painting on little designs over the past few years. I can say from experience: There is a lot of so-called easy nail art out there that, in reality, is very difficult to do yourself. But there are also plenty of designs you can totally pull off as a beginner. That, my friends, is what we’re here for today.

Unfortunately, the old “practice makes perfect” thing does actually apply to any type of creative endeavor, manicures included. I’m not saying you’re going to be able to perfectly recreate any of these looks the first time you try (though, if you can, maybe quit your day job). There’s a reason I still pay a professional to do my nails if I want them to look extra super good for a special occasion; those people have years of training I’ll never be able to catch up to. Instead, these are designs with elements I know a beginner can do well enough that you won’t get so frustrated with the results that you give up on your new hobby for good.

“It can be super intimidating to start painting your nails while you're looking at an inspo pic or video from IG or Pinterest, but remember that the reason you probably started painting your nails was to have a creative outlet and a moment of self expression,” nail artist and wise person Sigourney Nuñez says. “Try to not let perfectionism get in the way of something that is meant to be a self care moment where you get to show up for yourself in this fun, creative and colorful way.”

Hear, hear! If you’re ready to get started on your artistic ungual journey, keep scrolling for expert-recommended tips, tools, and plenty of design inspiration.

While your toolkit can grow exponentially the further you get into your nail art journey, Nuñez says the “perfect DIY tool trifecta” is a clean up brush, dotting tool, and liner brush.

I can confirm the clean up brush will be your most-used tool when you’re learning to do your own nails. Even if you don’t make a massive, colored-outside-the-lines mess, going around your nail with a clean up brush dipped in nail polish remover “can really give you that crispy salon finish at home,” Nuñez says. She uses a Simply Simmons #4 painter’s brush you can buy at Michael’s, but says you could also sacrifice an old angled eyeliner brush if you aren’t looking to spend money on tools yet. “Just be sure you don’t use it on your eyes again after you’ve dunked it in nail polish remover,” she warns.

Nuñez calls dotting tools “the gateway to the nail art universe.” You could use bobby pins, a flat eraser on the end of a pencil, or the end of a brush, but there are plenty of affordable dotting tool options too. Olive & June makes a nice double-sided one that would be a great first dotting tool, while Halo Taco has a fun, holographic set that comes with a total of eight different sized tips.

Once you’re really ready to level up, get yourself a nail brush. There are sets with multiple brush sizes out there, but Nuñez doesn’t think you need them. “I literally only use one type of liner brush, [the] Liner 3 from Bio Seaweed Gel,” she says. “It’s been my go-to brush for years.” Instead of dealing with a bunch of different size brushes, you can make different size lines by varying the amount of pressure you use.

I cannot emphasize this enough: If you’re serious about wanting your manicure to look as close to professionally done as possible, you absolutely must prep your nails before you even think about picking up your base coat. This means completely removing all your polish, cutting and/or shaping your nails with a file, and cleaning up your cuticle area... but not too much on that last part.

“When it comes to DIY prep, less is more,” Nuñez says. “There is no need to over trim any surrounding skin unless there's a hangnail that's waving hi at you.” Instead, she recommends using a cuticle buffer stick like the Sally Hansen Push & Buff 2-in-1 Cuticle Pen. “It helps exfoliate the cuticle area and side walls, plus helps buff out the waxy layer that can accumulate [on your nails],” Nuñez says. “That waxy layer is totally normal but it gets in the way of adhesion.” I like to wash my hands after I’ve done all that trimming, filing, and buffing, to make sure there’s no leftover nail dust that could ruin my hard work.

Keeping your cuticles healthy between manis will make this prep step easier. “I’m actually so passionate about cuticle care,” Brooklyn-based nail artist Anna Miles says. “A little attention every day will do wonders—more than any nail tech could accomplish in our monthly sessions.” She recommends using a towel to gentle scrub your cuticle area after a shower (”to encourage the cuticle skin to roll back”) and be diligent about using hand cream and cuticle oil after you wash your hands.

“If you’re like me and out of sight equals out of mind, buy travel sizes in multiples and keep one everywhere—bag, coffee table, bathroom, bedside,” Miles says. She loves the CND Rescue Rxx Daily Keratin Treatment and Zoya Healing Dry Skin Hand & Body Cream, both of which come in smaller sizes.

The number one thing that will make your nail art look better is to learn to leverage your fulcrum finger (aka the digit that’s holding your hand steady while you work; Nuñez says this is usually the pinky) and elbows. Do not try to do your nails while sitting on a couch; you need a table with enough space to create those stabilizing points.

Whether you’re going to use a dotting tool, liner brush, sponge, or other nail art tool, it can take some practice to figure out how much polish to put on it before you start designing. I always do a test run on a piece of paper; it’s not a perfect substitution for painting on your curved nail, but I think it gives you a pretty good idea of how much polish to use. Nuñez also has a handy video on how to load your liner brush properly.

And, again, you just need to practice. When I’m in the habit of doing my nails every week, I’m shocked at how much better I get over the course of a month. Ready to dive in? Here’s some inspo to get you started.

Let’s start with the easiest nail art of them all, a skittles manicure. This is simply painting each nail with a different shade of polish, which is a fun way to start playing around with color theory—a key skill for professional-looking DIY manis. “Every great manicure starts with a beautiful color story,” Nuñez says. “When the colors can do most of the talking, you don't have to try as hard with your DIY design.”

Glitter polish is kind of a nail art hack in its own right, because the texture makes any mistakes kinda blend in. For this look, I swiped some random splotches of purple cream over a glitter from Mooncat (a brand that makes some of the best sparkles, IMO).

The half and half manicure is a great way to practice creating straight lines: Since you fill in the space on one side of the line, it’s less obvious if things are little crooked.

“When painting lines, instead of moving the brush, you can try rotating your hand/nail while keeping the brush anchored,” Nuñez advises. “This can help with a more precise application.”

I know, I know, all these straight lines look intimidating, but, similar to the half and half, I promise that the fact that you fill in the triangles after you paint on the lines makes it far easier to get a crisp look. And if you have the patience, you could always wait for the base color to try, then use Scotch tape to stencil out the details.

Marble looks complicated, but Miles swears it’s not so difficult. “Marble is good for beginners because the success of this design is in the imperfection,” she is. “Uneven, shaky lines and blurry, smeared areas... sounds like the makings of a gorgeous marble!” Pick two colors of polish with high contrast to make the marble design stand out.

Miles says there are many ways to do marble, but one of the first methods she fell in love with is using an unused kitchen sponge that’s been cut into thin slices. Start by applying one of your polishes as a base coat, then paint the other color onto a nail-sized section of your sponge slice. “You can see the marble ‘veins’ on the sponge as you paint it,” Miles says. Stamp the wet paint onto your nail, using a rocking motion so it covers the whole nail. You can add metallic accents or a shimmery top coat to really make it special.

This is one of my go-to designs. You paint on two coats on your base color, then go in with a second color but stop before you get too close to your cuticle. It’s extremely simple, but gets loads of compliments.

If you crave more intricate designs but don’t have the brush skillset just yet, stickers are a great option. “I love using nail art stickers,” Miles says, noting that her favorites come from Deco Beauty. “For best results, make sure to apply them to a long-dried base color and paint two thin top coats instead of one thick one if you’re using traditional polish.”

Polka dot nails stay trendy, so that dotting tool is definitely worth the investment. There are many different ways to do dots—a bunch of super tiny dots, big circles, a mix and match of multiple size. I’d recommend practicing on a piece of paper first so you can get the pressure just right for the size you’re looking for. A light touch is key once you’re actually on the nail. “If your base color is not fully dry and you put too much force on the dotting tool that can create a dent and hole in your hard work,” Nuñez says.

This is a design that definitely requires Nuñez’s recommended stabilization techniques, but if things get a little shaky, you can just say you were going for a Bambi effect. Larger dots can also be a bit more forgiving.

Another trick for adding details when you don’t have a super steady hand is to apply the color you want for the detail as the base coat and then layer thick strokes of another color on top. That’s what I did for this look: The blue polish went down first, then I just painted one strip of green vertically and one strip horizontally to create the rectangle accent effect.

As everything gets digitized, I fear we’ll lose important ancient techniques, like using paper hole protectors as a stencil for a negative space half moon effect. At least, that’s how I’d pull off this look.

In the vein of nail stickers, confetti polish is a great way to create nail art without even looking at an extra tool. (KB Shimmer makes really fun ones that come in a bunch of shapes and colors.) Just make sure to apply two thin coats of top coat so the sparkles stay put.

I would never in a million years suggest that a French manicure is easy. But I will tell you that those rounded silicone stamper tools absolutely work to create a micro French, especially on shorter nails.

Using a rhinestone application tool can be a bit tricky to master, but I do find it a relatively easy way to add just a touch of detail to your nails. These won’t stick with just some good top coat; you’ll need to use nail glue too.

Great news: Once you’ve gotten good at polka dots, you can totally do flowers too. These are made up of just five black dots and one white one, but the final look seems much more complicated.

Kara McGrath is a contributing beauty editor at Cosmo with over 15 years of experience writing professionally about nails. She’s been an amateur nail artist for over 30.

Kara McGrath is a writer and editor with over 17 years of experience covering the beauty industry, women’s health, and a wide range of other lifestyle and wellness topics. After most recently serving as content director at Allure (2019-2025) and executive editor at Bustle (2014-2019), she is now fully freelance. In addition to Cosmo, her writing has appeared in Allure, The New York Times, Marie Claire, Business of Fashion, Playboy, NH Magazine, and more. Kara lives in New Hampshire with her dog, four cats, and a rotating cast of foster animals. You can find more of her work at karamcgrath.com.

#6854. Julia Child’s Purple-Hued Cambridge Home Sells Just Days After Listing

The legendary TV chef’s Massachusetts house fetched $6.525 million—above the asking price.

In today’s real estate market, price cuts are increasingly common. But Julia Child’s former Cambridge home just bucked the trend, selling mere days after listing—and fetching above the asking price. The legendary TV chef’s longtime Massachusetts residence hit the market on September 23 for $6.475 million. By October 2, it had found a buyer. The sale officially closed on October 29, per Zillow. In the end, the six-bedroom Cape Cod–style house traded hands for $6.525 million, $50,000 above asking.

Child and her husband, Paul, lived in the house for decades, starting in 1961, after Paul retired from his job in the federal government. The estate is also where Child shot several popular TV shows, among them In Julia’s Kitchen with Master Chefs and Julia and Jacques Cooking at Home.

Painted a muted shade of purple, the 6,300-square-foot home sits on a quarter-acre lot just a few blocks from Harvard Yard. Dating to 1889, the residence at one time featured a custom kitchen designed especially for Child, with pegboard walls, a six-burner industrial Garland range, and maple countertops a few inches taller than average to suit her 6’3” frame.

In the wake of Paul’s 1994 death, Child continued to live in the residence until 2001, when she donated the property to Smith College, her alma mater. (The Massachusetts institution sold the home in 2002 for $2.35 million, per Zillow.) The famous kitchen, meanwhile, was meticulously deconstructed—down to its linoleum flooring—and rebuilt in D.C.’s National Museum of American History, where it remains on display today.

Since then, the new owners have revamped the three-story home’s kitchen, which now sports marble countertops, a metallic backsplash, Poggenpohl cabinetry, and a Sub-Zero refrigerator. The rest of the residence is equipped with light-filled interiors, hardwood floors, and eco-friendly additions like solar panels.

This summer, the Cambridge City Council passed a proposal to install a historical marker on the estate’s grounds, according to Boston.com.

By chance, another Child-linked property recently traded hands as well, albeit for a discount. In August, a Santa Barbara ranch where she filmed the 12-episode PBS series Dinner at Julia’s listed for just under $22 million. It sold on October 16 for $19 million.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6853. Is Astrology Right or Left?

Like many aspects of American life, astrology is experiencing a political reckoning

In 1988, a former aide to President Reagan alleged that Nancy Reagan relied heavily on the astrologer Joan Quigley to guide key moments in her husband’s presidency. The Reagans denied that characterization, but the image of an easily swayed Nancy Reagan using some irrational force to push through Reagan’s policy decisions entered general pop culture, fueled in part by the misogyny of the political press toward most women in politics, left or right, in the 1980s.

The story of Joan Quigley and Nancy Reagan is part of a larger history of people turning to the stars to help explain, and potentially influence, political events. This is an impulse that doesn’t belong to any particular political ideology. But in current pop culture, astrology is often used as shorthand for liberal or leftist worldviews. Leading up to the 2024 U.S. presidential election, some online astrologers confidently celebrated a potential Harris win. Their despair over the actual outcome was reported in numerous outlets. It seemed to confirm the stereotype of who follows astrology: millennials who are deeply disturbed by the current state of the world.

Today, astrology, like weightlifting, holistic medicine, and organic farming, has become less a hobby and more a political signifier. So many practices that used to be read as lifestyle choices—tracking your macros, for instance, or taking up ceramics—have, in our hyperpolarized culture, become stand-ins for a person’s political leanings. But what’s curious about astrology is that it reads as liberal-coded to those outside the field and potentially conservative to those inside it.

In a 2022 piece titled “Astrology and the Search for Meaning” in The American Conservative, Bradford Tuckfield attributed the rise in astrology to the fact that cultural discourse had “damage[d] or destroy[ed] the …positive myths that used to animate people,” adding, “The patriotic idea of America as a great nation has been undermined by many efforts for decades, the most notable recent example being the ‘1619 Project.’ ” For conservatives like Tuckfield, astrology is an attempt to fill a vacuum left in the American psyche. The absence of religion and the demand that citizens grapple with uncomfortable historic truths supposedly left young people ideologically lost. “The myth of social justice seems to have absorbed much of the energy” of young people, Tuckfield claimed. And astrology is just like that “myth.” For conservatives, then, astrology is just one of many cultural challenges to a homogeneous culture built on the myth of the hero. Astrology, in their reading, leads to challenges to the status quo, which is, by definition, the antithesis of conservatism.

But that’s how outsiders view the field. Astrology also has its own organizational bodies that strive to define the discipline and often are administered by older, more politically and culturally conservative practitioners. But those groups’ influence over astrology has waned as it has become more accessible through the internet. Once-esoteric knowledge is now widely distilled through TikToks and live streams, and this has led to a political divide for many astrologers.

“To be able to study astrology historically has been a very privileged thing to do. And so it’s been dominated by white folks,” explains the astrologer Chani Nicholas. Nicholas is a popular astrologer who trained with those unaffiliated with traditional astrology organizations. “There’s very explicit reasons why I’ve not invested a tremendous amount of time in general astrology communities, even though I am massively indebted to the community and I have a deep appreciation for it,” she says.

Nicholas’s brand of astrology makes explicit connections between planetary transits and real-world political events. She directly cites progressive and leftist political goals in the context of astrology. Nicholas traces the political divide in astrology to post-pandemic America and links it to the same divide seen in alternative health spaces and yoga practices, where many practitioners became enthralled with MAGA and MAHA politics while others did not.

Before the pandemic, organized astrology was dominated by “a group of white people that are mostly able-bodied, mostly heterosexual, mostly cisgendered, that have never had to confront their own racism, sexism, homophobia, transphobia,” she notes. “In the pandemic, a lot of millennials started taking classes with a lot of establishment teachers, and a lot of the racism started to come out.… Millennials were like, ‘I’m not going to sit here and have you say that. You can’t say that.’ And then the more establishment teachers were like, ‘Why are you coming for me?’ ”

This tension between a more conservative old guard in astrology and a younger group of practitioners came to a head this summer when Stephen Forrest, an older practitioner of astrology, appeared on the Lila Astrology podcast. In the episode, he spoke about a satirical character he had once employed to make fun of racism, using the N-word in doing so. Forrest also brought up white-supremacist beliefs he had previously held on the inferiority of Black people’s intelligence; claimed to be related to Nathan Forrester, the founder of the KKK; and repeated the debunked myth that the KKK originally formed as a charity organization and not a terrorist one.

In response, younger astrology adherents asked that Forrest be removed as a speaker from an upcoming conference. Other older practitioners who were more deeply invested in astrology organizations cautioned against “cancellation.” Chani Nicholas, who was scheduled to appear at a conference that was also planning to host Forrest, pulled out of her appearance.

“Astrology attracts all these different types of people. It’s fascinating, but it doesn’t mean that because you’re interested in the human condition that you’re actually interested in interrogating your own biases and your own way of enacting violence on behalf of the system,” Nicholas says.

Astrology organizations, like many institutions currently, are caught between a younger generation, which is asking an older one to abide by the values of fairness and universal acceptance many purport, and older generations that are refusing to see the contradictions and biases in many of their organizations’ stances.

“There was no shared reality about where we had been working,” says Nicholas. “This younger generation of people that were really trying to understand identity and politics and location and power and privilege started to make this robust astrology community [of their own] and really push back against a lot of stuff that had just been existing unchecked for decades.”

Samuel F. Reynolds is an African American astrologer who, for more than 35 years, has chosen to work within astrological organizations. He originally came to astrology as a skeptic. One of the biases he pushes against is the tendency to ground all interpretations in Western culture. “When Uranus went into Libra, an older Western astrologer might say to you, ‘Oh yeah, that led to a time where there was an acceleration of divorces and where people started to gain more power related to marriage,’” Reynolds explains. “When they say things like that, I’m like, ‘Oh, okay, did that happen in Africa? And did that happen in India? Was that true in most of Asia?’ And the data actually is no, it didn’t happen during that same time frame.… It does bring up a philosophical question of what are we talking about and what are we looking at? Because the framework by which we talk about humanity is not quite as vast as astrologers like to believe when they talk about it. That’s the truth of it. You’re talking about the people who are like you or in some way who are adjacent to you.”

He remembers astrology conferences 20 years ago where “every other talk, someone would bring up the charts of the Beatles, Princess Di.” Astrology is an art of interpretation, which rests on the practitioner’s frame of reference. The limited references at conferences spoke to “the insularity in general of white people,” Reynolds says. He does note, though, that at a conference recently, he saw someone comparing the chart of Toni Morrison with Agatha Christie. “Why? I’m not exactly sure,” he jokes.

All of this conflict is happening in the wider context of big cultural and political shifts and the crumbling of any sort of authority or governing bodies. In extreme instability, the desire to understand why everything is changing makes astrology even more attractive. And astrology offers a lens for many to understand themselves. You may not be able to name why you have anxiety or why you tend to have chaotic romantic relationships or why you have trouble forming deep community bonds, but your planetary signature (and those of your friends and lovers) can provide you with something that looks like an answer.

But Nicholas cautions against giving too much sway to the question of transcendence and self-interrogation that astrology can sometimes provide. “I really want to talk about how we can build power and be effective now,” she says. “I don’t want to be out in outer space. I actually really want to be on earth.”

#6852. Kings of Leon’s Caleb Followill Is Finally Ready to Bare It All

The lead singer tells Esquire in the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned” that the band’s new record features its most honest music to date.

Last year, Kings of Leon was forced to cancel part of its summer tour. The band’s lead singer, Caleb Followill, broke his foot and severely displaced his heel during a freak accident that he jokes was “a rock ’n’ roll injury.” But really, he admits to Esquire, he was just playing tag with his kids—a little roughhousing that turned into eight months of recovery before he could walk back onto the stage.

“I was kind of feeling sorry for myself,” Followill tells Esquire editor in chief Michael Sebastian on the latest episode of “What I’ve Learned.” The singer felt like he had let the band’s fans down. He turned 44, he lost his mother not too long ago, and he channeled all that grief and negative energy into a new, stripped-down Kings of Leon record, O My Beloved, which was released on November 6. “I had a weight on my shoulders,” he says. “And one day I picked up the guitar, and it was almost like I walked through a door and I could see things a little more clearly.”

O My Beloved is the most melancholic and reflective album in Kings of Leon’s ten-album discography. Followill tells Esquire that he wanted to speak about his childhood and the family that shaped him on the new songs because he used to feel embarrassed about his past. His family grew up poor—traveling around the South and living out of their car at times. (Kings of Leon is a family band, with Caleb joined by his brothers Nathan and Jared Followill, as well as their cousin Matthew Followill.) But now Caleb wants to pass all his memories on to his kids, and he doesn’t want them to feel ashamed about any part of the journey that led them here.

“When I was writing these songs, my mind just kept going back to small moments in my life—moments that had no significance,” he says. “I wanted to tell these stories, and I wanted to be honest about parts of my life that I didn’t want to be honest about before."

O My Beloved is the band’s first self-produced record since parting ways with RCA, its longtime record label. It’s also the band’s tenth studio album, celebrating nearly 20 years since the 2008 record Only by the Night rocketed it to stardom thanks to hit songs “Sex on Fire” and “Use Somebody.”

“I was always scared of fame, and I think I still am,” Followill says. “That’s the good thing about having your brothers next to you. They’ll rip you right back down, shine a light in your eyes, and say, ‘Hey man, this can all come crumbling down and we’ll be back in our crumbling house.’ ”

You can listen to the episode on YouTube and Spotify. Elsewhere, Followill shares his secret to 15 years of marriage (to model Lily Aldridge) and what he’s learned from touring with Pearl Jam, U2, and Zach Bryan.

#6850. Artist Quentin Jones Launches a Surrealist Jewelry Collection

With Nocturne, the House of Quentin Jones founder brings the playful, sculptural language of her homewares to a collection of wearable art.

Above: Artist Quentin Jones wears pieces from her new jewelry collection while posing in front of tapestries Circe’s Garden and Western Moon.

British mixed-media artist Quentin Jones has quite the varied resume—one that spans artistic disciplines and collaborators as varied as Chanel, Apple, and Miley Cyrus. Now based in Brooklyn, her practice blends filmmaking, photography, collage, and painting.

Last year, Jones turned her eye towards interiors, launching a homeware line and design studio called House of Quentin Jones. The brand debuted with a collection of tapestries and ceramic sculptures that are both architectural and playful, leaning into Jones’s moody, dreamy look. Now, after a year of HOQJ, Jones is releasing new styles—and adding jewelry to the mix.

Nocturne, a collection of sculptural jewelry, translates Jones’s eclectic design language into wearable art. She pulled motifs like eyes, hands, and celestial forms from her tapestries and ceramics and incorporated them into the pieces. The collection features earrings, pendants, chains, and cuffs that balance black and white, pearl and onyx, organic and sculptural shapes.

“I’m interested in jewelry as sculpture,” Jones tells ELLE Decor. “A cuff wraps around an arm, a collar frames the neck, a tassel moves. The body becomes part of the composition. I want the pieces to have a presence of their own, while leaving room for the personality of the person wearing them.”

As she enters this new category, Jones is also beefing up her existing offerings. She’s adding new quilted tapestries to the studio’s repertoire and iterating on previous designs. With these new pieces, she says, “The palette has become softer and earthier—ivory, sand, and burgundy—but the compositions still have that surreal, graphic quality that runs through my work.”

But she isn’t stopping there. Jones has big dreams for the future of her eponymous studio, a “House” built larger with each new release. Next up, she’d like to expand the accessories category further, hoping to add bags, scarves, and other wearable pieces to her lineup.

“I’ve spent years working between art, film, and fashion,” she says. “I’m building towards a collection of things that can accompany you through your life—from what hangs on your wall to what you choose to wear.”

#6840. Jimmy Carter’s Family Farm Just Sold for $8.5 Million

The Georgia property will become part of a nearly 7,000-acre wildlife management area, with public access possible as early as 2027.

President Jimmy Carter’s family farmland near Plains, Georgia, was just sold to the Conservation Fund as part of an approximately $8.5 million acquisition that will protect nearly 2,500 acres of fields and woodland. Announced November 9, the deal sets the stage for the land’s eventual transfer to the state of Georgia, with public access potentially beginning as early as 2027.

The purchase encompasses approximately 1,600 acres owned by the Carter family, along with an adjoining tract. It does not include Carter’s boyhood home and farm, which are already preserved by the National Park Service as part of Jimmy Carter National Historical Park.

Instead, the newly acquired property is slated to join the nearby Lanahassee Creek Wildlife Management Area, expanding it to nearly 7,000 acres. The land provides habitat for gopher tortoises and other wildlife, while neighboring creeks support rare freshwater mussels. Plans call for the Georgia Department of Natural Resources to take ownership in 2028.

The acquisition also continues restoration work Carter initiated himself. At his request, the family preserved a stand of mature longleaf pine and replanted much of the surrounding acreage with the same native species. Once used to grow crops, including peanuts, the property became a retreat where he and Rosalynn spent time away from public life. The family acquired the land around the turn of the 20th century, according to the Conservation Fund.

“They liked to go and walk the land,” Blake Sullivan, who managed Carter’s farm and timberland for more than four decades, told The Washington Post. “For both of them, it was a place of rest and renewal.”

Carter continued visiting into his final years, arranging to be driven through the property when walking became difficult. Amy Carter, the president’s daughter, told The Post, “I think his very favorite place was to be in the town and on the land where he grew up, and where he had those very deep roots.”

#6838. I Asked Designers for Their Secrets to an Expensive-Looking Guest Bedroom. They All Said the Same Thing

Skip the fussy pillow pile and focus on the details guests will actually notice.

Pictured above: a bold aubergine double guest bedroom designed by Nicole Fisher.

The holiday hosting season is upon us, and while you’re planning your tablescape decor and seasonal trimmings, you might want to take a hard look at your guest bedroom.

“Most guest rooms get treated like overflow storage, and guests feel that the second they walk in,” says Marie Cloud, principal designer at Indigo Pruitt.

Even if you host only occasionally, your guest room should give visitors a comfortable place to unwind. No need for a guest book or fuzzy slippers: Designers say these four thoughtful touches can make the room feel luxe without costing a fortune.

Regardless of the design aesthetic, the ambiance of a room always hinges on lighting. “I always pay attention to the lighting,” says Louis Lin, founder of his eponymous New York City–based interior design studio. “A beautiful bedside lamp with a warm, flattering glow can transform the atmosphere of a room. Layering different light sources, rather than relying on overhead lighting, can do that. It's a relatively simple gesture that makes a room feel much more intimate and refined.” This also gives guests the freedom to tailor the air of their space to their liking. It’s one of the few details they can truly control in their temporary lodging.

While a pile of pillows might look inviting, designer Marie Cloud says the bedtime legwork promptly cancels out the aesthetics.

“Skip the stack of eight decorative pillows your guest has to relocate before bed,” says Cloud. Her perfect formula for a top-notch guest setup? Two sleeping pillows, plus one lumbar pillow, all set atop a good set of sheets.

“Sheets with real hand-feel, like a crisp percale or washed linen,” Cloud suggests. “Finish with a throw folded at the foot in a color pulled from the room. It looks considered, and it’s actually comfortable at 2 a.m.”

Most guest rooms get the all-white wall treatment in the name of neutrality, but all it really says is that your space lacks intention. Just because this room is (mostly) reserved for guests doesn't mean it has to lack character. In fact, Cloud says you should not hold back.

“Drench the room,” says the designer. “Walls, trim, and ceiling in the same color, ideally something deep and warm. Continuous color reads custom, it makes a small room feel wrapped rather than cramped, and it tells your guest the space was designed for them. A guest room is also the lowest-risk room in the house to be bold, so take the swing.”

The best guest setups make visitors feel anticipated, even when they show up unexpectedly. To achieve the boutique-hotel feel, designers recommend investing in small luxuries that leave a big impact. A few items Cloud and Lin always recommend having on hand are a small luggage rack, real hangers, fresh towels, and a select set of toiletries reserved especially for guests.

“The real luxury is making guests feel that the room has been prepared just for them,” says Lin.

#6834. The “Brainy Heel” Is This Season’s Shoe MVP

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

They’ll make every outfit feel more thoughtful

Sometimes, you just want to dress for yourself. It’s not about man-repelling so much as just responding to your own needs: I want to feel good, look well-dressed, and be drawn to what I’m wearing—for me, not them.

Looking at the new fall/winter 2026 shoes, from the feathered hiking styles at Prada to the peaked-toe lace-up shoes at Jil Sander to the grown-up Mary Janes at Chanel, I found myself coming back to that thought. These are heels for me, not them: attractive, but not designed purely to attract; chic, but not flashy; and, dare I say it, made with the wearer’s comfort in mind.

They’re shoes that make you think—and make you wonder what the woman wearing them is thinking too. You’re intrigued by her sartorial choices, especially her heels. Brainy heels? Perhaps. But like the woman herself, maybe they don’t need a title. Just a formula.

As with any well-designed object, what makes these shoes appealing is the law of proportions—the relationship between shapes that creates a visual balance. And what makes them compelling is that there’s always something just a little off, something to make you look again or make you wonder why, something that opposes and attracts at the same time. We’ve written about the wrong-shoe theory, the comfort-first commuter shoe, and plenty of the season’s chicest styles. The brainy heel brings them all together in one slightly off-kilter package.

Amanda added that she’s “fully present” for the awards season campaign, noting that “the amount of events and the amount of presence you need to have when campaigning for these things is pretty extreme—you saw me go through it last year—but it’s still all very positive because getting a seat at the table is the thing.”

She went on to explain that “A campaign is a lot. It’s time-consuming. It takes all your energy. And I’m totally willing to do it. A24 is great. They’re excited, and I’m very proud of the film, so I’m fully ready to engage in that. And I love these filmmakers! I had a great time with this tiny crew. And I think because [the experience] is so rare, and it will never happen again, I’m doubled-down on it.”

#6825. There’s Major New Evidence That Robert Pattinson Is the Next James Bond

Hardly a day goes by without a report dropping that someone’s cousin’s friend’s brother overheard someone else’s cousin’s friend’s brother at a bar say that [insert hot British actor here] is the next James Bond. And so far, Amazon MGM and director Denis Villeneuve have not confirmed one. single. thing.

However, we do know that several actors screen-tested for the iconic role, including Jack Lowden, Josh O’Connor, and—surprise!—Robert Pattinson. And according to Matthew Belloni in Puck News, there’s reason to think Robert is favored to become the next 007.

Turns out all potential Bonds sign contracts for the film and its follow-ups—and three separate sources told Belloni that while Jack and Josh “were required to sign up for four Bonds,” Robert “was able to lower that commitment to three” and negotiate himself “a whole lot more money than the others.”

So, what does that mean? Yet more sources explain that since Robert’s “test deal was so difficult to close,” people close to the situation are “wondering why the filmmakers would bother if they didn’t really want him.” Innnnnteresting!

There’s definitely some discourse about whether or not Bond should be an established household name (traditionally, he isn’t), with former casting director Debbie McWilliams—who cast the last three Bonds—telling The Independent, “It is absolutely essential that he retains a total enigma. I don’t want to see any of them as Bond because we now know so much about them. We want to know as little about them personally as possible, because that what spies are. We don’t need to know where he goes shopping or who his parents are, or where he lives. We never want to see him at home. And a vital element of the whole thing is his job description. He’s licensed to kill, and we have to believe that he can do that. If you don’t, then you’ve lost the audience.”

Casting Robert Pattinson definitely challenges that approach, but we’ll see!

#6819. I Paid a Matchmaker $250,000 to Find My Soulmate. The Result Surprised Me.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago.

Dating apps have become a special kind of hell for singles. People are sick of swiping. They’re tired of the road-to-nowhere conversations and disappointing dates. For all their predictive power, algorithms simply aren’t very good at finding the one.

As a result, people are dumping Tinder left and right. But they haven’t given up on love entirely. In fact, quite the opposite. After years of letting the apps play Cupid, they’re calling in the experts. Google searches for “matchmaker” more than doubled between January 2025 and January 2026. And as interest in matchmaking grows, prices are climbing too. Average fees jumped 26 percent last year. Prices vary enormously, but at the highest end of the market, finding a girlfriend can cost more than a Ferrari.

For the latest installment of “The Secret Lives of Men,” we spoke to Henry*, a 70-year-old man from Atlanta who spent $250,000 on a matchmaker. His fee covered a year of personalized matchmaking, complete with a wardrobe consultant, dating coaches, personality tests, and a nationwide search for eligible women. After eight months and roughly ten introductions, Henry finally met someone he liked.

*Name and identifying details have been changed to protect anonymity.

I’m what I would call a silver fox. I’m 70 years old now, and I’m in excellent physical, mental, and financial shape. I’ve been married twice and dated plenty of women. Over the years, I’ve tried just about every way to meet someone: bars, introductions through friends, dating sites, apps. But at this stage of my life, I can afford the very best—so I decided to shell out $250,000 for a top-of-the-line matchmaker.

Some people spend $250,000 on a car or $75,000 on a vacation in Positano. Others fly private. Sure, you could spend a fraction of that money, but you’d get a lesser experience. I look at matchmaking the same way. Sure, I could go on Tinder, swipe through profiles, and find women interested in me. But that’s a long process, and I don’t want to put in the same effort I did 20 or 30 years ago. I’m interested in finding someone I’m actually compatible with.

I’ve actually used cheaper matchmakers before, the kind who charge around $25,000 for a few months. Mostly they just save you the trouble of going through dating apps yourself. They’ll find a few women on Tinder, a few more somewhere else, and send you their profiles. This time I wanted someone who would actually get to know me and put some thought into whom she set me up with. That’s why I hired Sameera Sullivan. She works with only three or four clients at a time and charges $250,000 for a year of services that go far beyond what the other matchmakers offered. Those guys were like a Lexus. Sameera is a Bugatti.

The first thing she did was bring in a few professionals to help with my dating profile. A wardrobe consultant went through my clothes and suggested a few changes. A photographer took my pictures, and a professional writer put together my bio. Then I had several sessions with a dating coach and took a bunch of personality tests to figure out what kind of woman I’d be most compatible with.

Each date I went on helped Sameera narrow things down further. After every introduction, she’d ask both me and the woman she’d set me up with how it went. Even if we stopped seeing each other after two or three dates, she’d try to figure out why and use what she learned to make a better match next time. It’s kind of like AI. The more information she had, the better the results. The tenth woman she introduced me to ended up being the one. Sameera actually found her on LinkedIn and reached out to see if she’d be interested in going out with me.

We’ve been dating for almost three months now, and things are going extremely well. Everyone says we have big couples energy. She’s in her mid-50s, which is good because I look and act younger than my biological age. Our love languages match, our personalities complement each other, and I feel more compatible with her than I did with either of my former wives.

One thing I really like about her is that she’s got a bit of an adventurous streak. I’m naturally pretty steady and set in my ways, but I have an adventurous side too, and she brings it out in me. Like the other night, we were having dinner at a Mexican restaurant, and someone mentioned there was a speakeasy downstairs. Normally I would’ve said we should try it another time. But I saw her face light up, so I said, “Okay, let’s go.” We went in and had a drink. I probably wouldn’t have done that on my own.

I’ve put my matchmaking contract on pause for now. If this relationship doesn’t work out, I still have about four months of service left. For now, I’m very happy with how things are going. The funniest thing about it all, though, is that the woman Sameera found for me, the one she searched all over the country for—she actually lives in my neighborhood.

#6817. Designers Say These Are the Best Colors to Pair With Blue in Any Room

They perfectly complement the rainbow's most versatile hue.

A designer-favorite color to use in the home is beautiful, calming blue. Evoking natural elements like the sea and the sky, this serene hue can make any room feel more relaxed and welcoming, yet also more vibrant and bold, depending on the shades. And if this versatile hue is one you’re thinking about adding to your home, check out these beautiful colors that go with blue, proven by these designer spaces.

No matter your design style, this list of 38 colors that go with blue serves as evidence that the hue is as versatile as a neutral and can elevate any space you use it in. Read on to find your new favorite color scheme here.

To contrast the fanciful chinoiserie wallpaper in this San Francisco apartment, interior designer Caitlin Jones Ghajar and her team went bold and chose a warm yellow for the velvet bed frame. “This particular kind of blue lends itself really well to the vistas that are beyond, so you get some Bay view looks,” she says.

In this Amber Lewis–designed kitchen, the duck egg blue cabinetry is grounded by the rust red and cream floor tile. The hue, Light Blue by Farrow & Ball, can lean almost gray in soft lighting, making it a versatile neutral ready to pair with warm tones.

The deep, black accents in this kitchen add drama and depth to the slate blue cabinetry. The graphic color pairing is modern and masculine, while not trying to compete with the lush nature outdoors. Melissa Anderson of OAD Interiors designed this immersive space in Woodstock, New York.

Blue and yellow is a classic and cheerful color pairing. Elevate the duo by choosing a hue with a slightly greener tone, like a chartreuse. In this guest room by French & French, the cool light blue prevents a too-neon appearance.

Pairing a cool tone with a warm tone is a failsafe approach, and this bedroom by Serena Dugan does it expertly. A golden tan wallpaper softens the vibrant indigo accents on the headboard and window treatments. If you’re feeling bold, throw in some orange for added impact.

When you're unsure of what colors go with blue the best, simply opt for more of the same, as French & French did here. Perfect pattern play is achieved in this lounge through the matching blue and white palettes. A cobalt blue wall ties it all together.

For an impactful palette, add some glitz to your shade of blue. This living room, designed by Suzanne Kasler, utilizes metallics like brass and bronze to elevate the everyday hue to glamorous new heights.

In this Santa Fe home designed by the duo behind French & French, a rich, terra-cotta red balances the vibrant blue tones. To ground the swirling marbled wallpaper on the ceiling, the grand bookcases and coordinating rug (and sneaky chair piping!) incorporate the hue throughout the entire space.

In House Beautiful’s 2024 Whole Home, the guest room is cocooned in a mustard and navy color palette. Blue and yellow primary colors play off one another for a vibrant interior. Use neutral bedding to ground the bright shades.

Pink and blue can often feel like a baby shower or gender reveal, but take inspiration from designer Dane Austin to know how to use them correctly. By combining a pastel with a more vibrant tone, you can elevate the color pairing from juvenile to sophisticated.

Look to nature for hues to use with blue. This chic powder room, complete with a navy blue sink, is swathed in a floral green wallpaper. This color pairing is cheerful and can brighten up a smaller space, especially this one by Liz Carroll, which lacks windows.

Adorned in shades of cobalt and navy, the primary bedroom in our 2024 Whole Home in Asheville, North Carolina, continues the moody color palette with an ochre wallpaper. The hues are simultaneously subdued yet impactful, ideal for a serene night’s sleep.

A spa-like and timeless blue and gray color palette can brighten even the dingiest basements. To ensure nothing looks out of place, keep both hues at the same vibrancy level (e.g., a light blue and a light gray).

To add drama to this teal bedroom, designer Lisa Tharp opted for a totally red upholstered bed frame, and somehow it works. Blue and red are both primary colors, which means they pair well when one starts to lean toward the other’s complement (like the slightly green-toned blue walls).

In this playful kids' bathroom, a bright blue is the focal point color in the space. Looking to the color wheel for a clue, homeowner Ashley Maddox and designer Hilary Walker opted for a burnt orange shower curtain as the accent hue.

Pairing a myriad of cool-toned colors can create a sophisticated palette when done well, like in this Boston apartment. A purple rug complements the blue sectional sofa because they have similar textiles, tones, and brightness levels.

If you’re a fan of the blue and yellow primary pair, you’ll love this elevated option. Pairing a deep, rich blue, like this navy kitchen, with a metallic brass accent is a great way to add some glamour and texture to your space.

Monochrome color palettes can be really impactful when done with various shades of the same hue. Take this living room by Mark D. Sikes for example. The deep blue upholstery on the sofa and ottoman ground the lighter blue used to color drench the walls and ceiling.

Avery Cox of Avery Cox Design used a tonal blue for the wall to give this room an immersive effect and paired it with pops of bubblegum pink, purple, and citron yellow. The colors are the perfect pick-me-up when you're lounging indoors on a rainy day.

This robin's egg blue wallpaper is speckled with ivory and forest green to make daydreaming easy. The ivory mirror and green accessories designer Shannon Eddings added to this dining room are the supporting characters that soften the blue so it doesn't overwhelm the space.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6815. Rihanna Pairs Her Golden Jacquard Coat With a Pair of Baggy Blue Jeans

Another day, another Rihanna sighting at Giorgio Baldi. While most stars reserve their grandest looks for red carpets, galas, or film festivals, Rihanna’s preferred runway is the entrance to her favorite upscale Italian restaurant in Santa Monica, California. Over the years, the Fenty Beauty founder has used the venue to unveil some of her most-memorable looks—the sculptural white Alaïa set, the patchwork denim-and-leopard-print trench, the satin oyster-hued car coat with stacks on stacks of pearls. And she has yet to run out of ideas.

On Tuesday, Rihanna attempted a more under-the-radar visit, entering the restaurant through a tucked-away back entrance. And while her arrival may have been quiet, her outfit certainly wasn’t.

As one of the masters of high-low dressing, the “Umbrella” singer flexed her talents yet again, pairing an ornate, baroque duffle coat with a casual pair of baggy blue jeans.

Her oversize outerwear, courtesy of the Dries Van Noten Fall/Winter 2026 collection by Julian Klausner, was made with a golden floral jacquard that looked like a hanging tapestry transformed into wearable art. The piece featured a wide hood and toggle buttons in front that added a nautical spin, while around back, it featured a single vent and a martingale belt.

As a steadfast champion of a statement piece, RiRi chose a garment that was bold and fabulous, but could also help her attempt to remain incognito (a reality that’s nearly impossible when, well, you’re Rihanna).

The piece’s large hood and enveloping silhouette concealed the superstar, while in contrast, its gilded hue made her hard to miss. The coat made such a statement, in fact, that the mother of three knew she didn’t need to overwhelm it, which is why she paired it with a simple pair of barrel jeans and a gold collar necklace that complemented its metallic color. She finished things off with a pair of thin, rectangular sunglasses for a last low-key touch.

With this endorsement from Rihanna, and recent stamps of approval from other stars like Jennifer Lawrence, it appears Dries Van Noten coats are going to rule the outerwear game this season.
