#6784. Spice Up Your Mani with Cinnamon Brown Nails This Fall

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

At this point, we all know that a brown manicure is a fall staple. Even as I type this right now, I’m wearing one that’s a week old (in DND Spiced Brown, if you’re wondering). Espresso nails? Love. Chocolate brown nails? Yep. Chai latte nails? A lighter way to do things. But, it turns out we haven’t exhausted every way to wear the shade quite yet. The latest brown look to get on my radar is cinnamon nails, the warm, spicy take on the fall-friendly color. And honestly, with the holidays around the corner, the smell of cinnamon is sure to be wafting through my kitchen for the next couple months, so the timing couldn’t be better.

These warm, spicy nails combine rich brown with subtle red and copper undertones for a cozy, cinnamon-inspired shade that feels tailor-made for autumn. Ahead, with the help of celebrity nail artist Stephanie Stone (she’s Sally Hansen’s nail style director and has worked with lots of your favorite stars—ahem, Charli XCX) I’ll break down everything you need to know about trending cinnamon brown nails, including plenty of inspo pics, tips for getting the look, and polish recommendations.

As the name suggests, cinnamon brown nails are inspired by the warm, reddish-brown hue of the spice itself. It’s warmer than a classic brown and less red than a terracotta, sitting right in between the two. While espresso and chocolate brown nails tend to lean darker and cooler, these bring some toasty and earthy vibes to your fingertips. While a classic creamy polish looks great in cinnamon, the shade also offers plenty of opportunities to play around with the finish, like with a dimensional velvet cat eye manicure or a chrome topper.

The latter is one of Stone’s favorite ways to wear the trend—she reaches for Sally Hansen’s Miracle Gel in Chics in Charge to get the effect. “Think the glazed donut’s cousin coming to visit for winter break,” she says. And especially apt for the holidays, you can add a little dazzle to the shade: “If you want to give them some extra sparkle, add a crystal at the base of your nails for an extra catch of light,” she adds.

Stone puts it perfectly: “Every great manicure starts with immaculate cuticle prep and nail care.” Before reaching for any polish, “trim and file your nails to their desired shape,” she says. “Then use Sally Hansen’s Push & Buff Cuticle Pen to clean your nail plate of an excess dead skin.” This helps clean up your cuticles easily and quickly. Next, “wash your hands with soap and water and wipe your nail beds with alcohol,” she adds, noting how important this final prep step is, especially if you’re doing a metallic finish. “Metallic shimmer and polishes expose any texture or dust you may have on your nails,” she explains.

Then, it’s polish time. This part is pretty straightforward: Paint your nails with two coats of your favorite beige or brown nail polish, she says, recommending a medium brown shade “if you want your nail look to be reminiscent of a cinnamon roll” or a reddish brown “if you want your nails more reminiscent of cinnamon as the spice itself.” After letting dry for a few minutes, add top coat and cuticle oil to finish things off. Need some product recs? Stone’s go-to polish is Sally Hansen’s Miracle Gel Polish in Been There, Dune That, but you can also get the look with Olive & June’s Nail Polish in Sassy Molasses or Essie’s Gel Couture Liquid Diamonds in Marquise Please too.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmo. She’s covered and tested all things nails, from the coolest new polish colors to cuticle care and nail art trends.

Catharine Malzahn is a contributing beauty writer at Cosmopolitan and was previously the beauty assistant at Good Housekeeping, Woman's Day, and Prevention.

#6781. The Woven Tote Bag Trend Is Taking Over Runways and Workdays

Woven bags have officially reached icon status.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Is there any other bag that works as hard in real life as the tote bag? When it comes to being on duty and looking good, probably not. The humble tote has come in all iterations: lightweight cotton, supple leather, and suede. But more recently, the runways have been obsessed with making the oversize tote-style bags in luscious jumbo woven iterations. We saw them on the spring/summer 2027 runways at Dries Van Noten, Courrèges, Bottega Veneta, and Altuzarra. And then, labels like Bembien, Naghedi, and Dragon Diffusion are pumping out woven totes like there’s no tomorrow.

A big woven bag offers up texture and interest in a sea of ubiquitous canvas logo totes. It also gives you a bit of resilience so you don’t have to baby your bag on, say, a subway commute or a brisk morning walk where the risk of bumping into others is second nature. It is the working girl’s best friend. That’s just one of the reasons why fashion retailers have fallen head over heels for the style lately.

“Regardless of lifestyle, everyone needs a bag that carries a lot, and potentially the kitchen sink,” Ryan Kleman, Moda Operandi’s VP of buying, accessories, tells ELLE. The great news about a woven bag is that it also seamlessly goes with everything. Tone it down for a day at the office with a button-down top or a casual dress, or take it out for a nice dinner; a silk scarf tied about the strap can be the pop of color or embellishment you might crave for a more formal occasion.

In the case of some of these brands, the woven tote is literal fashion history. Bottega Veneta, for example, first introduced its signature “Intrecciato” woven leather technique in 1975. The fashion house’s spring/summer 2027 collection modernized the technique by applying it to enormous, hulking bags that swayed when worn over the shoulder. Paired with layered sweaters and matching leather belts, the bags felt like a decidedly fresh take on wearable artisanship by creative director Louise Trotter.

In the case of Dries Van Noten, one model carried an absolutely huge woven raffia-effect foldover clutch. Styled with a heather gray hoodie rolled up to reveal leather bubblegum-pink opera gloves with a biker-style cutout and buckle at the hand, the bag itself proved just how versatile a woven piece can be.

If you’re indeed thinking of upgrading your everyday work bag for a woven tote, it’s better to think bigger. “The oversized scale of the bags and the woven construction provide the perfect details to convert a chore into a fashion statement,” says Kleman. “At Moda, we have dubbed this trend the Big A** Bag and our customers are loving it.” This, too, is true: the bigger the weave, the better the carryall.

Bottega Veneta Pinacoteca Reversible Intrecciato Leather Tote

#6779. I Can Feel the Tremors of History Rumbling Beneath Scott Colom’s Victory

The senator-elect from Mississippi invoked Medger Evers in his winning speech, which reminded me of how far we’ve come—and how far we still have to go.

(Optional Musical Accompaniment to This Post)

Okay! Now we can celebrate. From the Mississippi Free Press:

Oh, to hell with the numbers. Let’s just celebrate a win by a guy cool enough to receive the good news of his election while wearing a Richard Pryor hoodie. From his victory speech:

One day in 1994, in the employ of another magazine, I went down to Jackson on assignment for a story about the death penalty that ultimately never ran. It was the day after the Republicans, led by Representative Newt Gingrich, won control of the entire Congress for the first time since 1952 and control of the House of Representatives for the first time since 1954. It can be argued, and I have, that these results scared the courage out of the Democratic party as thoroughly as George McGovern’s landslide loss to Richard Nixon in 1972 did. And when the modern Democratic party gets scared, it inevitably runs away from its most sacred commitments and the proudest moments in its history. This result caused Democratic president Bill Clinton to sign grotesque legislation like the Defense of Marriage Act and a crime bill that, relevant to my mission to Mississippi, added 28 new death-penalty crimes to the federal code. The bar in the Walthall Hotel, where I was staying, was filled every night by gloating Republicans, which drove me to room service for the balance of my stay.

On my last day in Jackson, I took a drive across town to what was then called 2332 Guynes Street in the Elraine subdivision, which had been developed in the 1950s by men named Winston Thompson and Leroy Burnett in order to service the rising Black middle class of the city. One of the houses in the development was purchased by Medgar and Myrlie Evers. On the night of June 12, 1963, Myrlie and their children were watching the speech in which President John F. Kennedy finally got off the dime on civil rights when they heard Medgar’s car coming up the driveway. The kids ran out to greet him. From behind a bush across the street, a back-shooting coward named Byron De La Beckwith sighted down his .30–06 Enfield and murdered Medgar Evers in front of his family.

I parked my car and walked slowly from De La Beckwith’s sniper’s nest to the driveway at 2332 Guynes Street, tracing the route of the bullet that killed Medgar Evers on that night in 1963. I was strangely aware of every step I took. I was deeply aware of the rise and fall of my feet, one after the other. It was as though there were a tiny tremor of history beneath me the closer I came to the spot under the carport where he fell, bleeding, before his family. It felt very much like a pilgrimage to some ancient shrine. There were sad and solemn bells tolling in my mind.

Listening to Scott Colom accept his victory on Wednesday night, as he name-checked Medgar Evers, along with Fannie Lou Hamer and Hiram Revels, I heard those bells again in my mind. Loud and raucous this time, they drowned out the crack of the whip and the crack of a .30–06 Enfield rifle fired with murderous intent. This time, they rang with joy, and the tremors of history I felt beneath my feet that day in 1994 felt like an earthquake.

#6775. Misha & Puff’s First Womenswear Collection Gives Knitwear a New Point of View

The cult-favorite children’s knitwear brand is officially expanding to adults.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Women’s fashion has some of the best designs out there—full stop. That said, every now and then I see a well-dressed kid and think, “Damn, I wish I had something like that.” This is especially true when I see a little one sporting a design from none other than Misha & Puff, a brand known for its ethically crafted, handmade, vintage-inspired children’s clothes. And while my wardrobe carries a playful touch, I’m not necessarily looking for a supersized version of a child’s sweater, but rather the essence of that whimsy. Thankfully, I don’t have to look too far; Misha & Puff just launched its first-ever women’s collection—and it’s more than I could have hoped for.

“Knits are hot. Knits are sexy. The way that they drape, the way that they accentuate your neck, I really do feel like we can do a lot with a knit,” Sophia Roe told ELLE.com over the phone. “For me, I feel really beautiful when I put these clothes on.” The James Beard Award-winning chef—along with her daughter, Mizu—came together with artist Mara Hoffman behind the lens to create a campaign that captures the essence of Misha & Puff, highlighting the craftsmanship and expression of the brand’s new womenswear collection.

“Everything I do with the brand is very authentic and really about what I’m interested in...That’s how the kids’ brand started, with me having my son and then growing up,” Misha & Puff’s founder, Anna Wallack, said on a phone call. “The choice to launch women’s in a bigger way really comes from the pull that I’m feeling as a woman. For me, dressing women and dressing myself is about how we’re working these pieces together. How do I feel in these silhouettes? Who is this character today? I want to keep playing with that in a bigger way.”

Wallack went on to tell me that designing for the collection—which launched today—started with what she described as a “fiber-first” approach. “We work with a Pima cotton in Peru that worked so well for kids because it’s incredibly soft and it’s so wearable, and then to find that I want to wear that all the time for myself; I want to wear our merino wool,” she explained. “Our decision to launch women’s with a bigger collection really was a commitment to doing it instead of just making one sweater here and there.” The result? A range of expressive knits in a color palette reminiscent of the ’70s that encourage the wearer to be who she is as a woman today. With every solid- or heather-hued piece there’s another featuring an eye-catching print or embroidery detail. “I feel a little more permission to explore other ideas in the adult collection and what it means to be a women, too.”

This exploration extends into Misha & Puff’s campaign. Roe shared on a phone call that the idea of partnering with Misha & Puff stemmed from just being a fan of the brand. “I said, ‘I don’t know what we can do but I really want to do something with Anna.’ Little did I know that in the background she was working on this unbelievable adult collection,” she said. Though this isn’t the first project Roe has starred in with her daughter, she explained it was the first where she was shot by another mother, making the experience feel truly special—especially as she comes into her body 15 months postpartum. “I feel kind of hot as a mom. There are a lot of narratives out there about getting back to whatever that pre-baby thing is, and I just kind of rebuke that in every single way. I think what I am now is kind of amazing and I really do feel like Mara was able to bring that out in a way that no one else could have.”

On the vision for the campaign, Hoffman shared that the focus was “seeing how does a woman exist through a space or on the street, and how does she live in it?” Working to capture Roe in a documentarian way, the artist wanted to make sure the imagery felt authentic to both the brand and the people wearing it. “We are lucky to be able to capture little shots of that along the way.” Of course, shooting with a baby can come with its own unpredictability. Luckily, as Roe shared, working with someone like Hoffman helped her relax on set with the reminder, “This is just life.”

#6772. ‘DWTS’ Frontrunner Amber Glenn Has Had to Make a Major Decision to Stay in the Competition

Competing for the Mirrorball means making some tough choices off the dance floor.

We all know Amber Glenn is a fierce competitor, thanks to her Olympic gold medals. So, it’s no surprise she’s one of the semifinalists on Dancing With the Stars season 35. But what fans might not know is how much she’s given up to keep pushing toward the Mirrorball trophy.

According to The Sun, Amber has stepped back from figure skating events to focus on the beloved reality competition. She was most recently removed from the entry list for the NHK Trophy in Japan, which kicks off later this month. It’s the sixth and final event in the Grand Prix regular season and the second figure skating event she’s opted out of at the last minute.

Amber also withdrew from this week’s Saatva Skate America competition in Everett, Washington, as she preps for the DWTS semifinals.

“For now, Amber is focused solely on DWTS, which has taken up most of her energy and time with grueling rehearsals and weekly live shows,” the outlet reports.

The Olympian is a frontrunner for this year’s coveted prize, up against Maura Higgins, Connor Wood, Harry Shum Jr., Ezra Frech, and Tatyana Ali.

During a November 12 appearance on The Zach Sang Show, Amber opened up about what it would mean for her to help her pro partner, Pasha Pashkov, win his first-ever Mirrorball trophy.

“That would mean the world because, and I’ve said this before, is that I came into this competition like, of course, who doesn’t want to win?” she said. “But it wasn’t like, ‘I need this because I’ve worked my entire life for it.’ Like, I’ve been dancing for two months. Yes, it has been a very intense, very passionate two months of working, but it has been his whole life that he has been dancing.”

She continued, “Imagine how much work I’m putting into it. He’s doing twice that amount because he has to choreograph. He has to do so many, like, visual selections. Like, there’s so much that the pro does for every single show that people don’t see.So yes, it’s about the celeb and who wins, but it also is about the pro.”

Though Amber has had to give up some skating for ballroom dancing, there’s a chance she’ll be back on the ice at the Prevagen U.S. Championships later this season in January.

In the meantime, we can’t wait to see her crush the DWTS semifinals next week!

#6769. A Behind-the-Scenes Look at Esquire’s Party for the 20th Anniversary of the Big Black Book

At the Ludlow House on the Lower East Side, a bunch of New York’s coolest people gathered to raise a glass to the essential guide to style and luxury.

It’s been two decades since the first edition of the Big Black Book debuted in 2006. Back then, a style guide devoted to the ins and outs of the luxury market wasn’t exactly common, but we believed it was necessary. The idea wasn’t to spam readers with a bunch of stuff they couldn’t afford but to give them a glimpse into the world of well-made, time-tested pieces that can create the backbone of a considered wardrobe.

Turns out, we were right. Men want to dress well, and they want to invest in the items—bench-made boots, bespoke tailoring, designer apparel—that look great on day one but even better a few years down the line. #6768. You're Shopping for Mattresses All Wrong—an Expert Says You Need to Try the "One-Roll" Test

It’s the best way to mimic how you’d actually use it at home.

Buying a mattress is not an easy decision, especially if you want a change from your current model or you’re upgrading to a different style to address aches and pains. The process feels high-stakes, which is why our editors are enthusiastic about testing and recommending mattresses, plus sharing any tips and tricks we learn along the way to prevent buyer’s remorse. Recently, we learned that picking out a bed simply based on its designated labels—twin, full, queen, king, and Cal king—may not be the most efficient way to buy a mattress.

Instead, Paul Keenan, store manager at U.K.-based brand Linthorpe Beds, suggested several sleep tests to gain more insight into buying the perfect mattress for your needs. “Most people know whether they sleep on a [full, queen or king-size mattress], but far fewer have ever checked whether those dimensions actually suit their body, sleeping habits and bedroom,” he notes. “Bed size is really easy to overlook because most of us shop using familiar labels like queen or king rather than thinking about the actual measurements we need.”

He goes on to suggest that your height, how much you move at night, and whether you share the mattress with someone else can all affect how much space feels comfortable. “There are some very simple checks you can carry out at home before deciding whether your current setup is right for you,” he says. Read ahead to try out his easy “tests” before you buy your next mattress.

This is a test you can try in stores if you’re testing the mattresses there, or at home if you’re doing a trial run with the mattress you just bought. These days, most brands offer at least 30 days of testing to see if you like the bed. To start, position yourself in the part of your bed you’d normally sleep on. Then, roll naturally into another comfortable position, such as from your back onto your side.

“Don’t deliberately tuck your arms or legs in to make yourself fit; the aim is simply to recreate the sort of movement you would make during the night,” says Keenan. “We move around far more in our sleep than we realize, so whether you can simply lie on a mattress isn’t the only thing to think about.

Most people, when testing a mattress in stores, just plop down horizontally and get up versus tossing and turning. The key is to shift your whole body into different positions, ideally with your partner next to you, to see if you both fit on the bed. If you sleep alone, you’re in the clear.

“If one normal turn takes you straight to the edge or into the person next to you, that can be a useful sign that the amount of usable space is more restricted than it feels when you’re lying still,” Keenan says. “It isn’t a strict pass or fail test, but it gives you another reason to look at the actual width you have available [versus relying on the categories of full, queen, king, etc].”

This one-roll test is helpful because, while mattress sizes are standardized, the physical size of two people sharing the bed changes how spacious a mattress can feel. Thus, what works for one couple may not work for another, so the one-roll test will specifically tell you if your mattress is too small, too big, or just the right size for your needs.

According to Keenan, there are several other tests you can try to determine if a mattress is right for you. One is called the 30-second “elbow test.” Both sleepers should lie on their backs, place their hands behind their heads, and allow their elbows to point naturally outwards. “If their elbows immediately touch or overlap, it may be a sign they would benefit from more width,” says Keenan. “It takes about 30 seconds, but it gives you a quick visual indication of how much personal space you actually have.”

Both the aforementioned tactics address width, but we can’t forget about height. As a general rule, add at least six inches to the tallest person in bed to ensure their feet do not hang off or press against a footboard. “The easiest way to check is to measure your own height against the length of the mattress, which will show exactly how much clearance is left between your height and the end of the bed,” says Keenan. “If you’re approaching six feet or taller, measure the mattress rather than relying on its label. You ideally want some room beyond your actual height, and the test takes less than a minute.”

Lastly, if you want a simple way to visualize how much space two people will actually share on the mattress, just place two standard pillows side by side on the bed. “The two-pillow test is a really simple way of putting the dimensions into perspective because most people have probably never thought about the width of their pillows in relation to the mattress underneath them,” Keenan explains. “If you already feel like you’re competing for space, seeing that comparison for yourself can make the dimensions much easier to understand. It doesn’t mean everybody sleeping on a double needs to size up, but it does show why the name of a bed size doesn’t necessarily tell you how spacious it will feel when two people are sharing it.”

Before you buy your next mattress, try these simple tests. They’re easy to do and could save you the headache of buying the wrong size for your sleep needs.

#6767. How to Layer Clothes for Fall and Winter Without Sacrificing Style

Fall is officially here and I am doing everything to stay warm. Layering is a very necessary skill come the colder months of the year. Not only is it practical—keeping you warm and such—but it also makes your fall and winter outfits that much more interesting.

As a Florida native now in the Northeast, layering is something I’m just now getting the hang of. And if you’re like me, either new to the cold or been here for years and can’t quite get it, I’ve got you covered. Below, I’ve gathered a few fall layering styling tips so you can lean into the fun of it all.

One of my favorite cozy pieces in my closet is an oversized turtleneck sweater. Not only will it keep you warm, but it is very chic. A fun way to elevate what could be seen as a simple look is tying your sweater around your waist, but specifically off to the side. It adds an undeniable touch of cool.

When first getting into layering, you can always start small. And doubling up on your shirts is the easiest way to achieve warmth. Raise the stakes by showing off those layers, either by wearing a longer shirt underneath or slightly raising the top shirt so the one underneath is visible. Even more points if the shirts are different colors so you get a color-blocking moment. Add in a peekaboo jacket zipped similarly to the look above and you’re set.

Belt layering is definitely more for the styling side of fall/winter layering, but it’s just as important. When trying to bring a little more life to a cold weather look, belt stacking is a fashion-forward option I personally love.

Oversized jackets are the real winners of fall. Especially a leather jacket like the one above. Add in a patterned sweater tied around the waist for a little more fun and you’ve got a perfect look.

Similar to tying around the waist, adding a scarf over top of your coat is another more style-forward layering tip that might not necessarily provide all the warmth, but brings the style.

A true Y2K favorite of mine that works great for the colder months is dresses over jeans. If you’re into nostalgia fashion, this is a more modern way to tap into the trend, while also staying warm.

#6766. Understated, Earthy, and Endlessly Wearable: The Best Olive Green Polishes

The earthy shade adds an edge to fall manicures while still wearing like a neutral.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Like all nail polish colors, green has a vast spectrum, one that runs from electric neon to bold kelly to soft sage and all the way down to deep, moody olive. Of all the greens out there, olive sits in a quieter place. The hue is earthy and a little mysterious, somewhere between a vintage army jacket and an actual juicy olive. It’s a shade we love for fall, since it pairs so seamlessly with black, rich browns, burgundy, red, and, of course, cream.

Once reserved for the brave few, olive has now gone mainstream. Part of the appeal is how wearable it is, offering an unexpected twist without ever feeling too loud. It’s also soft and soothing to the eye, making it an easy shade to wear day after day.

If you’re new to the shade, the easiest way in is to treat it like a neutral. Try it with a camel coat and chunky knits when the weather turns, or a crisp white shirt for something fresh and unexpected. A creamy, opaque finish feels polished and modern, while a glossy jelly or a hint of shimmer adds depth in low light. However you wear it, olive looks effortlessly considered and adds an edge to your style.

Below, we’ve rounded up the best olive green nail polishes to shop now.

A personal favorite, Elia is a muted olive green that looks rich from the very first stroke. The opaque formula glides on evenly in a single swipe and dries to a soft gloss that lasts through daily wear.

Manucurist’s take on olive green feels a touch brighter. It sits beautifully on light and dark skin tones alike and delivers an opaque wash of color. Part of the “Green Flash” collection, it cures under an LED gel lamp and lasts for nearly 21 days.

Essie’s Totally Plaid is an olive green polish with a cream finish that delivers a gel-like shine and depth while still removing as easily as a regular polish. The formula applies smoothly in two coats, giving you rich, opaque color without the need for a UV lamp.

This deep and moody olive green shade is the epitome of effortless chic, pairing easily with everything from neutral knits to bold jewel tones.

Inspired by the lush landscapes of the Mediterranean, this olive green evokes a lighter take on the olive color spectrum. It’s a tad brighter and slightly more yellow-toned, making it a great pick if you want a fresh, sunlit green this fall.

O.P.I’s Suzi is the chic olive green and wardrobe staple of the olive family. It’s a bit darker-toned, radiating earthy warmth and understated elegance. Plus, it’s fast-drying and offers up to seven days of wear.

For those seeking a little more sparkle, this olive green jelly is packed with large green holographic glitters that create a textured finish and catch the light with every movement, adding a spark of joy to your manicure.

Calming and serene, this moss green shade is part of the “Campground Collection”, a four-shade assortment of earthy, comforting colors. Its buttery-smooth application matches the shade’s peaceful look.

This may not look like your average olive green at first glance, but that’s because it has taupe undertones. Once it’s on your nails, you’ll notice the hint of green was there all along. The result is a soft, grounded neutral that pairs effortlessly with cozy fall knits and earthy accessories.

This nail color may be named Pickle, but we see the olive in there. This tangy green with muted yellow undertones feels lively while remaining neutral enough to pair with everything in your wardrobe.

A true olive green, Extra Dirty brings rich, earthy depth to your nails. All you need is one to two coats for a creamy manicure worthy of a fall-season spotlight.

#6764. This Historic Napa Valley Estate Features 18th-Century Charm, Fig Trees, and a Luxe Spa

Preserving the past while readying the property for a new chapter was the goal.

At Vine & Fig, each moment takes on a bit of everyday grandeur, spurred on by the sun-drenched warmth and crisp air of Napa County. The aptly named estate, set among vineyards, olive groves, and fig trees in St. Helena, California, has always had the makings of a particularly good gathering place; it just needed a little help catching up with the times.

Enter designer Jaime Rummerfield, who spent four years transforming the 19th-century property into a 10,500-square-foot retreat that feels both comfortable and reverential to its past. The undertaking was hardly a straightforward process. Between fires, floods, and the delicate logistics of preserving the estate’s historic architecture and geothermal streams, Rummerfield had to tackle the renovation with equal parts imagination and restraint. “You cannot approach a place like this as a blank canvas,” she explains. “Understanding what is already there becomes part of the design narrative.”

Fortunately, she had a willing creative partner in homeowner Olivia Marciano, whose background as artistic director of the Marciano Art Foundation brought an especially discerning perspective to the process. The two spent hours considering materials, palettes, and custom details, often allowing a single piece to inform the direction of an entire room. The resulting interiors are richly layered yet approachably livable: antique-style cabinetry with exposed hinges, hand-crafted furnishings, expressive stone, and artwork that lends the rooms a sense of collected history. “Everything shouldn’t feel as though it arrived on the same truck,” Rummerfield says.

Given that this is Napa Valley, it goes without saying that the perks of the property extend well beyond its walls. A chef’s kitchen opens onto the surrounding gardens, while the historic pool house was reimagined as an immersive hammam, complete with Moroccan mosaics, Taj Mahal stone, and copper hot and cold plunge tubs. But for Rummerfield, the property’s greatest luxury isn’t any single amenity, but the new chapter unfolding around it. “There are vineyards, olive groves, gardens, food being prepared, and people coming together,” she says. "The interiors are the stage and participate in all of that.” And at Vine & Fig, there's always room for one more act.

FAST FACTSDesigner: Jaime RummerfieldBuilder: JMA Uncommon BuildersArchitect: 4C Design GroupLandscape Architect: Surfacedesign, Inc Location: St. Helena, CaliforniaThe Space: A six-bedroom, ten-bathroom home across 10,500 square feet

Designed to accommodate everything from quiet family dinners to spirited parties, the kitchen embraces the property’s farm-to-table ethos. Antique-style cabinetry with exposed butt hinges lends the space a sense of history, while a white oak island, stone backsplash, and French trestle table keep things grounded. Beyond the windows, views of the mountains and gardens reinforce the connection between the home and its surroundings.

“We wanted the practical decisions and the decorative decisions to support one another,” adds Rummerfield. “The cabinetry details, hidden technology, ample storage, textiles, and connection to the outdoors keep it feeling like a room in a home. It is a chef’s delight, but it is also a very welcoming heart of the house.”

“We wanted this multi-use kitchen to have a personality appropriate to its setting while still feeling connected to the wider property, hammam, and pool,” says Rummerfield of this bonus cook space. “The proportions and material relationships become especially important. It ended up being one of our favorite spaces—the rich chocolate zellige tile back splash really made it unique and divine.”

Few amenities feel more appropriate to a Napa Valley estate than a dedicated wine cave. Here, Rummerfield let the architecture and natural materials establish the atmosphere, incorporating honed black soapstone countertops to create a space that feels equally suited to storing prized bottles and sharing a favorite vintage with friends.

Rummerfield bypassed a rigid furniture arrangement in favor of several intimate seating areas, creating a room that feels equally suited to lively conversation and afternoon lounging. “I wanted the living room to have a sense of occasion while remaining completely comfortable,” she says.

To that end, a generously proportioned, low-slung sectional offers a particularly inviting place to land, while custom cabinetry conceals modern technology, including a drop-down projector screen, without interrupting the room’s collected aesthetic.

“Passageways are wonderful places to create a moment of discovery,” Rummerfield says of the dramatic entryway and curved staircase, which features the client’s art colleciton. “I want these spaces to feel as personal, stylish and welcoming as the rooms they connect to.”

An arched ceiling and soft lighting sets the tone for the journey into the property’s reimagined pool house, where Rummerfield wanted to create “a completely different experience” within the property. “The hammam is about slowing down and enjoying the beauty of the materials,” she adds. “The barrel ceiling, Moroccan mosaic, and Taj Mahal stone give the space a wonderful sense of intimacy and ritual.”

“Powder rooms offer a lovely opportunity for personalization; the relationship between the vanity, sink, mirror, and light can give the room a distinct personality,” says Rummerfield. In here, she sources a Turkish sink from Olive Ateliers, incorporated a 1940’s glass sconce, inlaid an antique mirror, and brought the terra cotta paver floors from outside in. “Each element is deliberately chosen and work beautifully together,” she adds.

Affectionately dubbed the “Strawberry Room” after the pattern on the BDDW chair, this bedroom offers comfort and relaxation in space. Rummerfield leaned into the room’s cozy proportions with a tactile Phillip Jeffries wallcovering, layered fabrics, and sculptural lighting by Ken Fulk for Urban Electric.

“I like a bathroom to feel considered as a room in its own right,” says Rummerfield. “The vanity, mirror, lighting, and tub should have a pleasing relationship, with the materials doing much of the decorative work. The atmosphere should feel quiet and indulgent.”

“I enjoy giving utility spaces some personality,” Rummerfield says of the laundry room, which she coated in Benjamin Moore’s Spring Valley, a fresh green that nods to the surrounding landscape. “Many people spend time in them, so the light, finishes, and everyday experience deserve attention.”

Jaime Rummerfield is a Los Angeles–based interior designer known for her richly layered spaces that balance timeless elegance with a distinct sense of personality. Her work celebrates craftsmanship, architectural character, and the art of collecting, combining vintage finds, custom furnishings, and expressive materials to create interiors that feel both sophisticated and lived-in.

Montana politics never cease to amaze me. The state voted for Bill Clinton twice but has gone red ever since. It had a long and proud tradition of campaign-finance legislation until Citizens United turned the table. Like all timber and mining states, Montana has a long—and fairly unique—history of labor activity. Butte became known as the “Gibraltar of Unionism.” In 1914, two factions of the powerful Western Federation of Miners went to war with each other. (The dissidents blew up the union hall in Butte.) The recent election shifted things around within that state tradition.

Similar relief spread all over the Mountain West.

And it seems possible that there’s more to come.

Speaking of Ohio, Justice Jennifer Brunner is the only Democrat on the court right now. And she has been on the right side of some very bad decisions.

As a karmic reward, Brunner will be joined by fellow Democrat Marilyn Zayas, who beat an incumbent Republican.

We have seen the importance of state supreme courts in this dismal time. Many of them threw sand in the gears of Republican gerrymandering schemes. The effect of the Democratic gains in the states is going to be deep and vast. And Deb Haaland, one of the blog’s favorite people, is the new governor of New Mexico.

She lived long enough to see it all. God save the good work.

#6758. This Designer-Approved Curtain Trick Will Instantly Elevate Your Bedroom

Your window treatments should serve up both form and function.

Window treatments are often an afterthought, but your choice of drapery can make or break your entire design. Our 2026 Whole Home in Charleston is a master class in innovative design, and our designers took every opportunity to showcase their skills and style, down to the very last detail—including some gorgeous examples of transformative drapery.

Though often seen as a luxury, window treatments are a home necessity. Blinds, drapes, and shades don’t just line our windowpanes; they block drafts, light, and real-world clamor from entering your homes, practically on demand. While some choose to keep it simple, designers know your sill is uncharted territory for design potential. While it might be tempting to reach for your typical verticals, it’s worth making a statement. That said, good drapes can be expensive, so if you’re hoping to embrace your window treatment’s potential, you’ll have to budget for it.

In our patternmaxxed guest bedroom, designer Laura Lubin, founder of the Nashville-based design firm Ellerslie Interiors, used Stroheim drapes to add subtle color to her muted palette and elevate the chic TWOPAGES Roman shades just beyond. Then she took her design a step further: Instead of relying on pleating for texture, Lubin added a leafy trim and Fabricut bullion fringe to the edge of her panels, framing the bold blue baseboard and adding another note of texture to the room’s design.

Texture is meant to act as a bridge in design schemes, connecting elements and feeding both our visual and tactile appetites. In addition to her customized drapes, Lubin used grid-patterned wallpaper, natural wood grain, and plaid textiles to elevate the visual palette.

If you’re looking for a quick bedroom upgrade with big impact, it might be time to take a closer look at your drapes. After all, your window treatments are meant to block out the world, not your style.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

In a world where we’re hunched over our computers from nine to five, and then slouched and scrolling from five to nine, good posture is a rarity. But that doesn’t mean we should just accept a fate of poor spinal alignment. Especially when there’s actually a really easy solution (that doesn’t include deleting TikTok from your iPhone).

Allow me to introduce you to the posture-correcting bra. Along with delivering superior support, the shapewear category uses stretchy fabrics with varying levels of tension to reposition your shoulders and help you breathe better. Taylor Swift even wore one while training for the Eras Tour—and hers alone is low-key life-changing.

Now, even the best posture bras aren't intended to fix serious medical issues on their own, so it's not a substitute for, say, a scoliosis brace. But if you're looking for a little extra help retraining your body to sit up straight with your head held high, these types of bras encourage small corrections in the right direction!

A posture-correcting bra works best alongside an ergonomic chair, Pilates classes, or a new mattress, rather than as a replacement for any of them. It also happens to be the smallest investment of the bunch, and editors like me are big fans. Keep reading for my top picks—including the one and only Taylor Swift-approved option.

According to reviewers, it's comfy enough for every day and night (yes, even for wearing to bed if you so choose). Note that some say it runs slightly small!

Why we love it: Between the front closure and the padded straps, this wireless pick gives you more than one way to fine-tune your fit.

This full-coverage bra has a wide elastic underbust band and a high back to support your shoulders and keep you in alignment. Its front hook-and-eye closure adjusts at two levels, so you can loosen or tighten the band as needed, and the padded adjustable straps help prevent shoulder strain. Reviewers love how smooth it looks under clothes, especially along the back and sides. Note that many say the cups run small, and the brand recommends sizing up for a more relaxed fit!

Why we love it: This lightweight brace is meant to be layered over a bra, so you get posture support without giving up your go-to everyday bra.

Instead of replacing your bra, this thin nylon-spandex brace slips on like a vest and wraps around your shoulders and upper back to gently pull them into alignment. A wide hook-and-loop belt fastens around your rib cage, so you can adjust how snug it feels, and it’s low-profile enough to wear under a blouse or sweater. Reviewers say it’s comfortable enough for long workdays at a desk, and some also wear it over a tee or cami at home. Note that a few say the shoulder straps can dig into their underarms after several hours, so you might want to ease into wearing it!

Why we love it: Sometimes posture-correcting bras, especially the more intense ones, can be hard to wear for extended periods of time. This one is more lightweight, if you're hoping for an option that can be worn all day, every day.

This posture corrector is less of an over-the-shoulder boulder holder and more of a bralette. Will it lock your shoulders into place like some of the more heavy-duty options on this list? No, probably not. But it’s lightweight and a lot more comfortable, so you can wear it for longer periods of time and reap the posture-correcting benefits while lounging. The thick straps have hook-and-eye closures that allow you to adjust the length!

Why we love it: This option comes in a wide range of sizes for a variety of bust types. It doesn't restrict your breathing or get uncomfortable throughout the day, either.

One of the best benefits of this posture-correcting bra? The bust and shoulder support combo that it offers will help you relieve boob-induced back pain while keeping your spine aligned. Glamorise is already such a tried-and-true brand for well-constructed bras made for anyone with a larger chest, so get ready to be impressed by this one—especially when you see that it's comfy enough that you're not itching to fling off your bra once you get home.

#6756. Zimmermann Hosts a Star-Studded Soirée in Downtown New York City

Katie Holmes, Uma Thurman, and Naomi Watts came out to celebrate the brand’s cruise 2027 collection.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Inside Indochine’s red-lit, retro-inspired French-Vietnamese dining room in New York City, Zimmermann gathered friends of the brand to celebrate its cruise 2027 collection. Presented on the sun-drenched Côte d’Azur in June, the label’s latest served as the perfect bit of vacation and occasion-ready dressing for November in New York. Now that the lineup has officially hit stores, fashion insiders and fans of the brand alike (including Uma Thurman, Eve Hewson, Katie Holmes, and Naomi Watts) showed up dripping in ruffled gowns, sailor-inspired stripes, chunky shearlings, and ethereal lace and silks from the collection. Against the dark, 1940s-inspired tropical salon interior of Indochine, it was the ideal mix of downtown energy and New York glamour.

As champagne flowed through the bar area, glasses clinked while miniature crab cakes and spring rolls were passed. Zimmermann’s sister duo Nicky and Simone Zimmermann could be spotted catching up with friends new and old. “We have a very, very close connection with New York,” Nicky told ELLE in between greeting guests. “It was pretty much when I started the business, one of the first places internationally that I went to in the ’90s, and it blew my mind. It’s been inspirational to us 100 percent.” It was a rare occasion for the brand; for years, Zimmermann has shown at Paris Fashion Week (after presenting in New York in the mid-2010s) and the duo oscillates between Sydney and Paris for most of the year.

Zimmermann’s painterly and intrinsically bohemian flourishes could be seen in the many outfits of guests, all highlighting the vivid cruise collection. Think: decadent silky draped drop-waisted gowns and paneled lace maxi dresses, or, for the more demure, sleek all-black looks that captured the New York essence. “For me, there’s a little bit of a sense of humor behind it—without insulting anybody—it was about the story when Australia actually beat America in the America’s Cup, which is a very famous sailing race,” Nicky told ELLE of the collection’s inspiration.

“I think it was in 1983, and America had not lost the America’s Cup in something like 173 years; it was a ridiculous winning streak, and Australia came along. The inspiration is more about a David and Goliath kind of story,” she continued. “We came along as the Aussies, not established in the sailing world. They were very cheeky, rogue, kind of Aussie young guys up against this amazing, sophisticated establishment of the New York Yacht Club. And they never lost—and we beat them. So in Australia, it’s a legendary story.” Nicky combined the sportiness of sailing gear with the sort of elite New York Yacht Club preppiness, resulting in colorful silk scarf embellishment or sweaters decked out in accessorized pins. “They would hold the most incredible parties and the women were so glamorous and sophisticated and beautiful and not anything that Australia could relate to at the time, so I loved the contrast.”

Conversation was flowing and blooming anthuriums covered the tables as guests took their seats. Nicky kicked off the dinner with a speech, musing on her early days in New York on the Lower East Side and the many parties she attended in Tribeca before sharing more inspiration behind the collection: “It’s about establishment, and then it’s about people, you know, working really hard, coming together as a team, defeating the undefeatable,” she said, with a laugh.

Next came an intimate performance by the singer and Bad Monkey actress Charlotte Lawrence, who sang two of her original songs, then led a folksy sing-along to “Dreams” by Fleetwood Mac. Even the most guarded of celebs and fashion’s elite couldn’t help but bob their heads and sing a tune. As guests dined on a family-style mix of kale salad, spicy shrimp, grilled salmon, barbecued chicken, and steak, the champagne glass clinks slowed as candles slowly flickered and the evening closed out with lemon tarts.

A little yacht-club polish, a little Aussie irreverence: Zimmermann celebrated cruise 2027 in high style.

#6755. Jennifer Lawrence Swaps Her Pops of Lemon for a Polished Dark Monochrome Look

Complete with a glossy Hermès crocodile Kelly

Jennifer Lawrence can just as easily pull off a bright lemon-yellow Colleen Allen coat or a pair of funky sequined pink Siedrés pants as she can a polished, monochromatic business look.

Earlier this week, the actor looked so chic in an outfit composed of timeless navy and black staples. The base of her look was made up of an elongated, buttoned blazer, and matching trousers. To continue the color scheme, she wrapped a navy scarf around her neck.

Then came the luxurious black accents: square sunglasses and a rare Hermès Kelly Sellier in shiny crocodile leather and gold hardware. And because Lawrence cannot live without her pops of color, the actor slipped on a pair of burgundy velvet slippers.

Lawrence is often one to go minimalist with her jewelry and glam, and she did that here, opting for polish-free nails, minimal makeup, and a few gold rings (one of them being her engagement ring from husband Cooke Maroney).

As you may have guessed, the cherry on top of the look was Lawrence’s dirty-blonde hair, which was styled down in easy, breezy long waves. The Hunger Games alum recently introduced her haircare brand, Mes, and has since been promoting her products around town.

“I feel like if my hair looks good, no one will notice anything else about my face, my clothes, or my body,” the actor joked in a recent interview with The Cut.

Of course, we disagree. While we do love a good hair day, we’ll never miss a J.Law street-style moment.

#6754. 12 12 12 Shakes Up the Tired Heist Formula with One Great Idea

Can the Anthony Mackie-led crime drama pull it off?

“A high-asset robbery, professionally conceived, is twelve months in planning, twelve hours in execution,” says Anthony Mackie’s character, FBI special agent Jack Strickland, at the start of the new Apple TV crime drama, 12 12 12. “A bank robber who is not caught within twelve days is not caught at all.”

It’s an amazing premise—honestly, one of the best I’ve heard in a long time. The first episode takes the viewer one month into planning the heist, one day into pulling off the heist, and one hour into their attempted escape. Then, episode 2 will move along to two months, two days, and two hours. Neat, right? But the big question for cocreators Jim Keeble and Dudi Appleton’s time-shifting heist series remains the same as any seemingly impossible bank job: Do they have the right team to pull it off?

From the two-episode premiere—now available to stream on Apple TV—I’d say we’re off to a very strong start. 12 12 12’s opening scene pays homage to Ocean’s 11’s Shaobo Qin when a contortionist unfolds his body and climbs out of a large safety deposit box. It’s one of the most genius ideas I’ve seen in a heist story of the last few years—I mean, talk about a man on the inside—especially in an overcrowded field of complex heist series. Remember Netflix’s Kaleidoscope, which allowed viewers to watch the story starting on any episode in the series? Well, 12 12 12 is far more than just a gimmick.

The story follows FBI special agent Jack Strickland (Mackie), who has tracked down one of the world’s best bank robbers, Paul Galvin (Jamie Dornan), to Zurich, Switzerland. Galvin and his unruly brother Bobby (Jack Kesy, who was last seen playing Robbie Pedergrast’s late brother on Task), made an enemy of Strickland when the FBI agent’s partner was killed as she attempted to arrest them during a major heist in Newark, New Jersey.

Now, Strickland has a personal vendetta against the man. He throws his philosophy that you’ll never catch a bank robber after twelve days of the robbery out the window—which is fine because the creators fabricated the whole “12 12 12” FBI rule anyway just to provide episodic framing for the series. (Maybe it resets now that Galvin is planning another heist?) Either way, 12 12 12 does a great job of making sure you never get lost in the three timelines.

Strickland’s white whale is also far from a one-note character himself. He’s a man following in his father’s footsteps to get out of a life of poverty, which would be easier if his brother Bobby wasn’t such of a wild card who can’t be trusted unattended for a single second.

As it turns out, Bobby gambled away all their money from the Newark heist, including Paul’s half. So his brother’s debtors force Paul back into a life of crime under threat of violence to his new lady love in France, his father-in-law, and their unborn child. It’s also quickly revealed that our contortionist is just one of the interesting members of Paul’s team, who are all tasked by Bobby’s mysterious benefactors with robbing a Zurich bank of 800,000 Swiss francs all owned by the Russian mafia.

From the first two episodes released so far, the 12 12 12 team seems like it has the right men for the job. The timeline-shifting format allows the series to do much more than simply break the action to provide a little backstory. As we move from months ahead to days after, and vice-versa, we also switch perspectives that allow for Paul and Jack to trade who is the hero and who is the villain.

Jack can be both the righteous FBI agent pursuing the bank robbers in the present and a man clearly far too hellbent on revenge in the past. The same goes for Paul, whose team blackmails, honeypots, and occasionally murders throughout the heist, while his backstory paints him as a good man who was forced down a dark road.

Where 12 12 12 falls short, at least so far, is in the script. Kari Skogland (who previously worked with Mackie on The Falcon and the Winter Soldier) directs the two-part premiere handily enough, but Keeble and Appleton’s writing is very formulaic. Scripts don’t always matter when you’re working with amazing characters like the contortionist lockpick. True Detective: Night Country’s Kali Reis is also on the team. Her mission is to hack into the subway system so that they can make their escape on public transit, which, of course, goes wrong. But when you have so much dialogue doubling as exposition in between the action, it feels like everyone’s conversations are more for the viewer than they are for each other.

For now, I’m hopeful that all the kinks are worked out once we move past the who, what, and why of 12 12 12’s opening episodes. Given the gimmick, and the fact that the series is only eight episodes instead of twelve, I’m excited to see just what throws the entire concept of the series into chaos.

#6753. 8 Popular Home Items From 20 Years Ago That Are Making a Surprising Comeback

They may be a blast from the past, but they’re about to be very now.

Trends come and go, but they almost always come back again. If there’s one thing we know about interior design, it’s that what feels old and outdated manages to become fresh and cool again in a few years’ time. Skeptics can easily be proved wrong with the resurgence of sunken living rooms, glass block walls, and even cushy sofas plucked right out of a Nora Ephron movie. And, as younger generations continue to be intrigued with the 1990s and early 2000s, they are turning to the era’s interiors for inspiration.

Well, sort of, as Maggie Vollrath of Provision Studios points out. “When we look back at certain interiors from the 2000s, there was such a commitment to achieving a particular look that every element in the room was chosen to reinforce it,” the Georgia designer says. “A home should feel like a collection of things you genuinely love, not as though you’ve walked onto a movie set designed to represent a particular style.”

When you cherry-pick trends and design details from the past, you’re able to create a home that honors the past without appearing stuck in the past. “The goal isn’t to make a home look like it belongs to another decade,” Vollrath says. “You want to bring back the beauty and character of those decorative details while creating something that feels authentic to both the house and its owners.”

Below, designers share trends from the past that the next generation of design lovers adore.

For older generations, a swivel chair might conjure memories of sitting in your family’s den, but designers promise these movin’, groovin’ seats have been revived for the 21st century. “Swivel barrel chairs defined early-2000s transitional interiors, usually in a bright flat polyester-blend upholstery,” explains Hallie Malitsky, principal designer of Hallie Rose Design in Brooklyn. “The version coming back today is a different experience entirely.”

Today’s iterations, she insists, are subtler. Cheap polyester has yielded to rich, tactile fabrics like wool, linen, and velvet. For a timely twist, she also encourages trading the fire engine red and butter yellow popular in the 2000s for burgundy and ochre. “Surround it with natural materials like wood, linen, and stone, and [your chair] will stop being the loudest thing in the room,” she recommends.

When Malitsky thinks of early aughts design, she immediately envisions the gigantic walnut media center that plagued many of her friends’ family rooms. “Dark wood cabinetry is a notorious culprit of bad 2000s design, but the wood wasn’t the issue,” she says. “It was the fact that there was so much of it.”

Now that dark wood tones are making a comeback, Malitsky says to use them sparingly. Two to three walnut or ebony pieces per room will make enough of a statement. And, no: Unlike the living rooms of yore, your furniture doesn’t have to match.

“What I love about their return is that we’re incorporating these pieces in a much more collected way,” shares Vollrath. “Rather than purchasing an entire matching furniture collection, we’re mixing periods and bringing in individual pieces that have character and history.”

The early aughts had lots of elaborate cabinetry, replete with corbels, substantial cornices, and beautifully detailed range hoods. As everyone—yes, even younger generations—continue to favor more traditional interiors, we’re seeing a renewed interest in ornamented cabinetry.

“There’s something really special about a kitchen that feels as though it could have been there for generations, or even reminds you of your grandmother’s kitchen,” Vollrath says. “Those decorative flourishes bring a sense of history, craftsmanship, and permanence that can be difficult to achieve with a more streamlined approach.”

But why stop there when you can add character to other rooms of the house?

“We’re seeing a broader interest in architectural detailing throughout the home, from wainscoting and wall paneling to ceiling beams and substantial moldings,” she adds. “Homeowners are becoming more interested in understanding these elements, considering them during renovations, and making them a priority in new construction.”

If the rise of analog interiors proves anything, it’s that even the best chatbot can’t beat excellent design. Instead of having a home covered in screens and smart systems, many are favoring more primitive setups that harken back to dial-up and CD-ROMs. For example, Fargo-based designer Lindsey Christie has been noticing (and loving) the return of landlines. “There's something wonderfully nostalgic about a beautiful telephone sitting on a bedside table or tucked into a thoughtfully designed corner of a home,” she says.

Of course, there’s plenty of fun to be had in a “dumb home.” “We have had several adult clients wanting to incorporate their hobby of Legos into their design aesthetic,” reveals Allison Smith of Gem + Clay Interiors in St. Augustine, Florida. “I definitely think this speaks to a desire to return to the analog, and that [old-school] element.”

“We always joke about the Tuscan kitchens from 2005, but they had some good things going on,” says Drew Michael Scott of Lone Fox. If you strip away the “bottles of preserved peppers and gaudy oil-rubbed bronze faucets,” the style was actually rocking a great color palette. Shades like brown, ochre, terracotta, and deep red bring a little bit of the great outdoors inside, plus they can pair nicely with today’s cozy interiors. But let’s keep the overly Italian influences in the past, shall we?

Today’s emerging design lovers are extra—just ask Scott, who has been noticing a considerable uptick in passementerie. “These intricate details were everywhere in the mid-2000s, before the world decided everything should be sleek and gray,” he says. “They’re becoming popular again, and I think they really evoke the attention to detail we saw at the turn of the century.”

Though yesteryear’s tassels, beading, and decorative trim are undeniably fun, using them requires a little bit of strategy. Since there’s a fine line between charming and kitschy, balance these louder, upholstered moments with pared-back wooden pieces.

Goodbye, panel-ready refrigerators and dishwashers: Katie Kiser says stainless steel appliances are so back. According to the Tennessee-based designer, the resurgence is a break from the picture-perfect kitchens older generations have pined over. “Gen-Z values practicality over perfection,” she explains. “I think it will show up in their interiors.” And why stop at appliances when you can bring that lived-in feel to the rest of the space? “They don’t hate clutter the way millennials do,” she adds. “We’ll see details like cookbooks in the kitchens and hall trees with tennis rackets on them.”

Though Scott hasn’t seen the Venetian Gold granite that ruled Y2K just yet, he’s noticing many homeowners are willing to get creative with their countertops.

“We've moved beyond using only Carrara marble in kitchens,” he explains.

Ditto for quartz, which some designers believe is on the outs. Instead, Scott says travertine is huge again and granite is poised to make the comeback. If you want to keep these materials from appearing dated, he encourages you to reach for stones with bolding veining and inclusions.
